 succeeded in 288ms:
     1: \section{Higher-state fibres: recalled realization theory and a quantitative orbit theorem}
     2: 
     3: The minimal-realization statements in this subsection are recalled background,
     4: not contributions of this paper.  Kalman's similarity theorem is the classical
     5: source, and minimal MAP/phase-type formulations and canonical-coordinate
     6: developments are given in
     7: \cite{Kalman1963LinearSystems,TelekHorvath2007MinimalMAP,
     8: BladtNielsen2017MatrixExponential,MeszarosTelek2013DiscreteMAP2Canonical,
     9: PappTelek2013DiscretePHCanonical}.  We state the consequence needed to locate
    10: the visible fibre, then prove the new quantitative assertion: at a minimal
    11: interior killed-reset kernel the admissible fibre has local dimension
    12: \((n-1)^2\).  Nonunique MAP and Coxian representatives are documented in
    13: \cite{RamirezCoboLilloWiper2010MAP2Nonidentifiability,
    14: RamirezCoboLillo2012WeakEquivalentMAP,RizkBurkeWalsh2019CoxianNonuniqueness}.
    15: Graph-specific time-to-event inverses are treated algebraically by
    16: Radulescu et al.\ \cite{RadulescuEtAl2025TimeToEventIdentifiability}; the
    17: criterion below instead isolates the exact orbit fibre for any declared
    18: minimal killed-reset subclass.
    19: 
    20: \paragraph{Recalled minimal-realization uniqueness.}
    21: Let \((a,A,b)\) and \((\widetilde a,\widetilde A,\widetilde b)\) be two real
    22: scalar realizations of the same dimension \(n\) such that
    23: \[
    24: aA^k b=\widetilde a\widetilde A^k\widetilde b
    25: \qquad(k\ge0).
    26: \]
    27: Assume that both reachability matrices
    28: \[
    29: [\,b,Ab,\ldots,A^{n-1}b\,],\qquad
    30: [\,\widetilde b,\widetilde A\widetilde b,\ldots,
    31: \widetilde A^{n-1}\widetilde b\,]
    32: \]
    33: and both observability matrices
    34: \[
    35: \begin{bmatrix}a\\aA\\\vdots\\aA^{n-1}\end{bmatrix},
    36: \qquad
    37: \begin{bmatrix}\widetilde a\\\widetilde a\widetilde A\\\vdots\\
    38: \widetilde a\widetilde A^{n-1}\end{bmatrix}
    39: \]
    40: have rank \(n\). Then there is a unique \(M\in\operatorname{GL}_n(\RR)\)
    41: such that
    42: \[
    43: \widetilde a=aM,
    44: \qquad \widetilde A=M^{-1}AM,
    45: \qquad \widetilde b=M^{-1}b.
    46: \]
    47: These rank hypotheses are precisely properties \textup{(R)} and \textup{(L)}
    48: of Bladt and Nielsen, whose Theorem~4.2.16 characterizes minimality by their
    49: simultaneous validity; the corresponding intertwining relations are given in
    50: their Lemmas~4.2.5--4.2.6
    51: \cite[Theorem~4.2.16, p.~216, and Lemmas~4.2.5--4.2.6,
    52: p.~212]{BladtNielsen2017MatrixExponential}. This standard result is invoked
    53: here and is not claimed as a new theorem; see also Kalman
    54: \cite{Kalman1963LinearSystems}, Telek--Horv\'ath
    55: \cite{TelekHorvath2007MinimalMAP}, and
    56: \cite{Ryden1996Aggregated,OCinneide1989PhaseTypeNonuniqueness,
    57: OCinneide1991InvariantPolytopes}.
    58: 
    59: \begin{proposition}[Recalled orbit-fibre consequence of minimal realization]
    60: \label{thm:nstate-killed-reset-identifiability-dichotomy-main}
    61: Fix \(n\ge2\), put \(\beta=e_n^\top\), and let
    62: \[
    63: \mathcal K_n=\{K\in\RR^{n\times n}:K\ge0,\ K\mathbf1\le\mathbf1,\
    64: \operatorname{spr}(K)<1\}.
    65: \]
    66: For \(K\in\mathcal K_n\), define the killed-reset D-MAP by
    67: \[
    68: T_0=K,\qquad T_1=(I-K)\mathbf1\beta
    69: \]
    70: and its Palm survival coordinates by \(S_k(K)=\beta K^k\mathbf1\).
    71: Call \(K\) minimal when both
    72: \[
    73: \mathcal C(K)=[\,\mathbf1,K\mathbf1,\ldots,K^{n-1}\mathbf1\,],
    74: \qquad
    75: \mathcal O(K)=
    76: \begin{bmatrix}\beta\\ \beta K\\ \vdots\\ \beta K^{n-1}\end{bmatrix}
    77: \]
    78: have rank \(n\). Let
    79: \[
    80: \mathcal G_n=\{M\in\operatorname{GL}_n(\RR):
    81: M\mathbf1=\mathbf1,\ \beta M=\beta\},
    82: \]
    83: and let \(\mathfrak P_n\subset\mathcal G_n\) be the reset-preserving
    84: permutation matrices.
    85: 
    86: For any declared subclass \(\mathcal C\subset\mathcal K_n\) of minimal
    87: kernels, fix the structural equivalence \(\sim_{\mathcal C}\) under which
    88: identification is requested, and assume that \(K\sim_{\mathcal C}L\) implies
    89: equality of visible laws. Examples are reset-preserving hidden-state
    90: relabeling and, in a serial rate chart, permutation of the rate coordinates.
    91: Define the Markovian orbit fibre
    92: \[
    93: \mathfrak F_{\mathcal C}(K)=
    94: \left\{M^{-1}KM\in\mathcal C:M\in\mathcal G_n\right\}
    95: \big/\!\sim_{\mathcal C}.
    96: \]
    97: Then standard minimal-realization uniqueness gives the following exact
    98: characterization.
    99: \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
   100: \item Two minimal kernels \(K,L\in\mathcal K_n\) generate the same stationary
   101: visible click law if and only if
   102: \[
   103: \begin{aligned}
   104: L&=M^{-1}KM &&\text{for the unique }M\in\mathcal G_n\\
   105: &&&\text{implementing their normalized minimal realizations}.
   106: \end{aligned}
   107: \]
   108: \item Equivalently, the declared hidden object is identifiable from the visible law within
   109: \(\mathcal C\), modulo \(\sim_{\mathcal C}\), if and only if
   110: \[
   111: \#\mathfrak F_{\mathcal C}(K)=1\qquad\text{for every }K\in\mathcal C.
   112: \]
   113: Pointwise identifiability at \(K\) is equivalent to the same condition at
   114: that \(K\); if the cardinality exceeds one, its distinct classes are exactly
   115: the hidden nonidentifiability fibre. Thus this assertion is the orbit-fibre
   116: reformulation of part \textup{(i)}.
   117: \end{enumerate}
   118: Part~\textup{(ii)} is deliberately recorded only as terminology: after the
   119: fibre has been defined, singleton fibre and identifiability modulo
   120: \(\sim_{\mathcal C}\) are the same assertion.  Neither part is claimed as a
   121: new realization theorem.
   122: \end{proposition}
   123: 
   124: \begin{proof}
   125: Deterministic reset makes the visible process renewal. Its Palm gap tail is
   126: exactly \(S_k(K)\), so equality of visible stationary laws is equivalent to
   127: equality of all the scalar coordinates \(\beta K^k\mathbf1\), \(k\ge0\).
   128: Apply the minimal-realization theorem stated above to the triples
   129: \((\beta,K,\mathbf1)\) and \((\beta,L,\mathbf1)\). Its hypotheses are exactly
   130: the two displayed rank conditions in the proposition, and it gives a unique
   131: similarity \(L=M^{-1}KM\). Preservation of the fixed input and output vectors
   132: gives \(M\mathbf1=\mathbf1\) and \(\beta M=\beta\), respectively.
   133: Conversely, these two identities give
   134: \[
   135: \beta(M^{-1}KM)^k\mathbf1=\beta K^k\mathbf1
   136: \]
   137: for every \(k\), proving (i). Taking the intersection with
   138: \(\mathcal C\) and then quotienting precisely the declared structural
   139: equivalence proves
   140: the necessary-and-sufficient assertion (ii).
   141: \end{proof}
   142: 
   143: \begin{corollary}[All-collision serial identifiability]
   144: \label{cor:nstate-serial-all-collision-identifiability-main}
   145: Fix \(n\ge2\), a known \(\Delta\tau>0\), and arbitrary positive rates
   146: \(\theta=(\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_n)\), with repetitions allowed. Let
   147: \[
   148: Q(\theta)_{ii}=-\theta_i,\qquad
   149: Q(\theta)_{i,i-1}=\theta_i\quad(2\le i\le n),
   150: \]
   151: with all other entries zero, and put
   152: \[
   153: T_0(\theta)=e^{\Delta\tau Q(\theta)},\qquad
   154: T_1(\theta)=(I-T_0(\theta))\mathbf1e_n^\top.
   155: \]
   156: Then the visible record identifies exactly the unordered rate multiset
   157: \(\{\!\{\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_n\}\!\}\). Equivalently, the fibre of the
   158: labelled serial parameter is its \(\mathfrak S_n\)-permutation orbit.
   159: 
   160: More explicitly, if the distinct rates are
   161: \(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r\) with multiplicities
   162: \(m_1,\ldots,m_r\), and \(z_j=e^{-\lambda_j\Delta\tau}\), then the Palm tail
   163: has the confluent Prony form
   164: \[
   165: S_k=\sum_{j=1}^r\sum_{\ell=0}^{m_j-1}
   166: c_{j\ell}k^\ell z_j^k,\qquad c_{j,m_j-1}\ne0,
   167: \]
   168: and its minimal recurrence polynomial is
   169: \[
   170: \chi_\theta(x)=\prod_{j=1}^r(x-z_j)^{m_j}.
   171: \]
   172: The leading \(n\times n\) Hankel matrix
   173: \((S_{i+j})_{0\le i,j<n}\) is invertible; hence
   174: \(S_0,\ldots,S_{2n-1}\) determine \(\chi_\theta\), including all collision
   175: multiplicities. Pole collision therefore changes conditioning and the local
   176: root chart, but does not change population identifiability.
   177: \end{corollary}
   178: 
   179: \begin{proof}
   180: Starting in phase \(n\), the absorption time is
   181: \(W=X_n+\cdots+X_1\), where the \(X_i\) are independent exponentials with
   182: rates \(\theta_i\). Its Laplace transform is
   183: \[
   184: \EE e^{-sW}=\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{\theta_i}{s+\theta_i}.
   185: \]
   186: Consequently
   187: \[
   188: \int_0^\infty e^{-st}\PP(W>t)\,dt
   189: =\frac{1-\prod_{i=1}^n\theta_i/(s+\theta_i)}{s}
   190: \]
   191: has at
   192: \(-\lambda_j\) a pole of exact order \(m_j\). Partial-fraction inversion and
   193: sampling at \(t=k\Delta\tau\) give the displayed exponential-polynomial form
   194: with nonzero top confluent coefficient. Standard confluent Prony algebra
   195: then gives the exact minimal annihilator \(\chi_\theta\)
   196: \cite{KunisRoemerVonDerOhe2020Prony}.
   197: 
   198: For completeness of the finite-coordinate assertion, the matrices
   199: \(\mathcal O(Q)\) and \(\mathcal C(Q)\) are nonsingular: successive rows
   200: \(e_n^\top Q^j\) and successive columns \(Q^j\mathbf1\) expose the nonzero
   201: bidiagonal products triangularly. Because the exponential is injective on
   202: the real spectrum of \(Q\), Hermite interpolation supplies a polynomial
   203: \(p\) with \(Q=p(e^{\Delta\tau Q})\); hence the corresponding observation and
   204: reachability spaces for \(T_0=e^{\Delta\tau Q}\) are also full. Therefore
   205: \[
   206: (S_{i+j})_{0\le i,j<n}=\mathcal O(T_0)\mathcal C(T_0)
   207: \]
   208: is invertible, and the first \(2n\) tails solve uniquely for the order-\(n\)
   209: recurrence. The roots and their algebraic multiplicities recover
   210: \(\lambda_j=-\Delta\tau^{-1}\log z_j\) and \(m_j\). Conversely, permuting
   211: the independent summands does not change \(W\), the Palm gap law, or the
   212: stationary renewal law. This proves the exact fibre claim.
   213: \end{proof}
   214: 
   215: \begin{theorem}[Quantitative interior fibre dimension and positivity persistence]
   216: \label{thm:nstate-interior-continuum-nonidentifiability-main}
   217: For the unrestricted class with \(\sim_{\mathcal K_n}\) equal to
   218: reset-preserving state relabelling, the hidden kernel is locally
   219: nonidentifiable at every minimal interior point \(K\), where interior means
   220: \(K>0\) and \((I-K)\mathbf1>0\) entrywise. More precisely, the
   221: reset-preserving similarity group has Lie algebra
   222: \[
   223: \mathfrak g_n=\{B:B\mathbf1=0,\ \beta B=0\},
   224: \qquad \dim\mathfrak g_n=(n-1)^2,
   225: \]
   226: and the orbit map
   227: \[
   228: \psi_K:\mathcal G_n\longrightarrow\mathbb R^{n\times n},\qquad
   229: \psi_K(M)=M^{-1}KM,
   230: \]
   231: has injective differential
   232: \[
   233: D\psi_K(I)B=[K,B]\qquad(B\in\mathfrak g_n).
   234: \]
   235: Consequently the visible fibre through \(K\) contains, in a neighbourhood of
   236: \(K\), a smooth immersed manifold of dimension exactly \((n-1)^2\).  Every
   237: kernel on a sufficiently small neighbourhood of this orbit manifold remains
   238: strictly substochastic: its entries and killing deficits are positive.  After
   239: quotienting by the finite reset-preserving permutation group, the local fibre
   240: still has dimension \((n-1)^2\).
   241: \end{theorem}
   242: 
   243: \begin{proof}
   244: Because \(\beta\mathbf1=1\),
   245: \(\RR^n=\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf1\}\oplus\ker\beta\).  Relative to this
   246: decomposition, \(B\mathbf1=0\) removes the first block column and
   247: \(\beta B=0\) forces the range into \(\ker\beta\).  Thus
   248: \(B=\operatorname{diag}(0,A)\), with arbitrary
   249: \(A\in\operatorname{End}(\ker\beta)\), and
   250: \(\dim\mathfrak g_n=(n-1)^2\).
   251: 
   252: Differentiating \(\exp(-tB)K\exp(tB)\) at zero gives
   253: \(D\psi_K(I)B=[K,B]\).  Suppose \([K,B]=0\).  Since \(B\mathbf1=0\),
   254: \[
   255: BK^j\mathbf1=K^jB\mathbf1=0,
   256: \qquad j=0,\ldots,n-1.
   257: \]
   258: The reachability matrix
   259: \([\mathbf1,K\mathbf1,\ldots,K^{n-1}\mathbf1]\) has rank \(n\), so these
   260: equalities imply \(B=0\).  Hence the differential is injective on all of
   261: \(\mathfrak g_n\), not merely in one selected direction.
   262: 
   263: The constant-rank theorem now makes the image of \(\psi_K\) near the identity
   264: a smooth immersed manifold of dimension \((n-1)^2\).  For every
   265: \(M\in\mathcal G_n\),
   266: \[
   267: \beta\psi_K(M)^j\mathbf1=\beta K^j\mathbf1\qquad(j\ge0),
   268: \]
   269: so the recalled orbit-fibre result places this entire manifold in the visible
   270: fibre.  Finally, all entries of \(M^{-1}KM\) and all components of
   271: \((I-M^{-1}KM)\mathbf1\) depend continuously on \(M\).  Their values at
   272: \(M=I\) are strictly positive; a sufficiently small identity neighbourhood
   273: therefore remains inside the interior Markovian cone.  The stabilizer has zero
   274: Lie algebra by injectivity of the differential, and quotienting by the finite
   275: reset-preserving permutation group cannot change local dimension.  This proves
   276: all assertions.
   277: \end{proof}
   278: 
   279: \begin{remark}[Nonminimal and graph-specific open interfaces]
   280: \label{rem:nstate-identifiability-open-interfaces-main}
   281: The characterization and the two headline results deliberately assume
   282: Hankel/minimal rank \(n\). If a nominal
   283: \(n\)-state representation has lower visible rank, equal laws need not be
   284: related by an invertible \(n\times n\) similarity; a sharp classification then
   285: requires a stratified treatment of nonminimal augmentations. For a fixed
   286: nonserial zero pattern on the boundary of the Markovian cone, evaluating the
   287: orbit intersection \(\mathfrak F_{\mathcal C}(K)\) is an exact semialgebraic
   288: problem, but no graph-uniform closed form is claimed here. Finally, collision
   289: multiplicity is population-identifiable in the serial model but statistically
   290: singular under noisy finite records. These are explicit interfaces, not
   291: consequences of the present results.
   292: \end{remark}
   293: 
   294: For orientation, the unrestricted fibre is already nontrivial in two states:
   295: \[
   296: K=\begin{pmatrix}1/2&0\\1/2&1/4\end{pmatrix},\qquad
   297: M=\begin{pmatrix}9/10&1/10\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
   298: M^{-1}KM=\begin{pmatrix}9/20&1/45\\9/20&3/10\end{pmatrix}.
   299: \]
   300: Both kernels are nonnegative and substochastic,
   301: \(M\mathbf1=\mathbf1\), \(e_2^\top M=e_2^\top\), and their entire visible
   302: renewal laws coincide, although the hidden kernels do not.

  1360: numerical rate set
  1361: \[
  1362: \left\{-\frac{\log p}{\Delta\tau},
  1363:        -\frac{\log s}{\Delta\tau}\right\}
  1364: =\{\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r}\}.
  1365: \]
  1366: If \(\Delta\tau\) is not supplied as a known design interval, the visible law
  1367: stops at the unordered dimensionless product set
  1368: \[
  1369: \{-\log p,-\log s\}
  1370: =\{\Gamma\Delta\tau,\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\}.
  1371: \]
  1372: Its root discriminant is
  1373: \[
  1374: \Delta_\sigma:=\Phi_1^2-4\Phi_2=(p-s)^2 .
  1375: \]
  1376: Within this constrained D-MAP(2) subfamily, the population inverse is
  1377: the quotient map
  1378: \[
  1379: r\longmapsto (p+s,ps).
  1380: \]
  1381: This theorem stops at the exchange quotient; ordered
  1382: \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\) requires
  1383: \Cref{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}.  The first population singularity
  1384: is the quotient denominator
  1385: \[
  1386: \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}=r_1-r_0^2=r_0(a-r_0),
  1387: \]
  1388: which must be nonzero to recover \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  The second is
  1389: not a singularity of the quotient inverse itself: at
  1390: \(\Delta_\sigma=0\) the unordered pair simply collapses to the common root.
  1391: Only the separated-root labelled chart of
  1392: \Cref{prop:population-separated-root-labelled-chart-main} is singular there.  The
  1393: dimensionless formulas
  1394: \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}--\eqref{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main} show
  1395: that the stationary visible law generated by \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\)
  1396: equals that generated by \((\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Gamma)\).  Thus the visible
  1397: law identifies only the exchange quotient, not an ordered branch labelling.
  1398: At the exchange diagonal
  1399: \(\sigma=(2z,z^2)\), \(0<z<1\), the unordered pair collapses to the single
  1400: survival factor \(z\), so the common dimensionless product \(-\log z\), and
  1401: for known \(\Delta\tau\) the common numerical rate \(-\log z/\Delta\tau\),
  1402: remains identifiable even though no separated ordered branch is.
  1403: \end{proposition}
  1404: 
  1405: \begin{proof}
  1406: First identify the only cancellation in the inverse.  From
  1407: \[
  1408: r_0=\rho,\qquad r_1=\PP(A_0=A_1=1)=\rho a,\qquad
  1409: r_2=\PP(A_0=A_2=1)=\rho u_2
  1410: \]
  1411: and \(r_0>0\), one obtains \(a=r_1/r_0\) and \(u_2=r_2/r_0\).  The Palm
  1412: covariance identity \Cref{lem:covariance-transfer-main} gives
  1413: \[
  1414: u_2=\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}},
  1415: \]
  1416: so, since \(r_0=\rho\),
  1417: \[
  1418: r_2-r_0^2
  1419: =\rho\{\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\}-\rho^2
  1420: =\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}},
  1421: \]
  1422: while
  1423: \[
  1424: r_1-r_0^2=\rho a-\rho^2=\rho(a-\rho).
  1425: \]
  1426: For the constrained D-MAP, \(\rho>0\) and \(a-\rho<0\) by
  1427: \Cref{lem:stationary-main}.  Hence
  1428: \[
  1429: r_1-r_0^2=\rho(a-\rho)\neq0,
  1430: \]
  1431: and the quotient division is by the nonzero scalar
  1432: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}\), not by a quantity that may vanish.  Therefore
  1433: \[
  1434: \lambda
  1435: =\frac{u_2-r_0}{a-r_0}
  1436: =\frac{r_2/r_0-r_0}{r_1/r_0-r_0}
  1437: =\frac{r_2-r_0^2}{r_1-r_0^2}.
  1438: \]
  1439: The final equality uses only scalar division by \(r_0>0\) and
  1440: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}\ne0\), precisely the stated inverse domain.
  1441: 
  1442: The two coordinates of the boxed map are now computed directly.  Since
  1443: \(a=1-s-b\) and \(\lambda=\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\),
  1444: \[
  1445: \Phi_1=1-(1-s-b)+(p-b)=p+s.
  1446: \]
  1447: For the second coordinate, write \(q=1-p\).  Also
  1448: \[
  1449: 1-\lambda=1-p+b=q+b,
  1450: \]
  1451: and the stationary click probability from \Cref{lem:stationary-main} is
  1452: \[
  1453: \rho=\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}.
  1454: \]
  1455: Therefore
  1456: \[
  1457: \rho(1-\lambda)
  1458: =\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}(q+b)
  1459: =q(1-s).
  1460: \]
  1461: Since \(r_0=\rho\), substituting this value, \(a=1-s-b\), and
  1462: \(\lambda=p-b\) into the boxed formula gives the full scalar identity
  1463: \[
  1464: \begin{aligned}
  1465: \Phi_2
  1466: &=\rho(1-\lambda)-a+\lambda\\
  1467: &=q(1-s)-(1-s-b)+(p-b)\\
  1468: &=q(1-s)+p+s-1\\
  1469: &=q-qs+p+s-1\\
  1470: &=s-qs\\
  1471: &=ps .
  1472: \end{aligned}
  1473: \]
  1474: These are exactly the two identities summarized in
  1475: \Cref{lem:quotient-inverse-symmetric-algebra-main}.  Hence
  1476: \[
  1477: \{p,s\}=\text{roots of }z^2-\Phi_1z+\Phi_2=0.
  1478: \]
  1479: The unordered roots are therefore identified, but their labels are not.  If
  1480: \(\Delta\tau\) is fixed by design, applying
  1481: \(z\mapsto-\log z/\Delta\tau\) to these roots gives the unordered numerical
  1482: rate set; without that known interval the root set gives only the
  1483: dimensionless products \(\{-\log p,-\log s\}\).  Finally,
  1484: \Cref{lem:gap-law-exchange-symmetry-main} proves that swapping
  1485: the two roots also leaves the stationary renewal gap law, and hence all
  1486: finite-dimensional visible distributions, unchanged.  The inverse therefore
  1487: lands on the quotient by the exchange \((p,\Gamma)\leftrightarrow
  1488: (s,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\), not on separated labelled rates.  At a double root
  1489: the quotient itself contains the common survival factor and dimensionless
  1490: product, with the common numerical rate obtained only for known
  1491: \(\Delta\tau\), as made explicit in
  1492: \Cref{lem:diagonal-common-rate-main}.
  1493: \end{proof}
