  1495: \begin{definition}[Projected plug-in word-law reconstruction]\label{def:projected-plugin-word-law-reconstruction-main}
  1496: A finite-record projected plug-in word-law reconstruction is a consistency
  1497: construction formed from a noisy inclusion triple only on the retained event
  1498: \(\mathcal E_N^{\rm law}\) fixed in advance.  That event is a deterministic
  1499: domain-of-definition and withholding rule inside
  1500: \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}, not a model-validation test and not
  1501: an uncertainty statement.  On that retained branch, the raw quotient coordinate
  1502: \(\Phi(\widehat r_N^{\mathrm{cyc}})\) is first projected to
  1503: \[
  1504: F=\{(x+y,xy):0\le x,y\le1\}
  1505: \]
  1506: by the tie-broken nearest-point projection
  1507: \(\widetilde\sigma_N=\Pi_F^{\rm tb}\{\Phi(\widehat r_N^{\mathrm{cyc}})\}\).
  1508: The reconstruction is the lifted renewal-consistent coordinate
  1509: \[
  1510: (\rho_*(\widetilde\sigma_N),\widetilde\sigma_N)\in
  1511: \mathcal M_{\rm ren}
  1512: :=\{(\rho_*(\sigma),\sigma):\sigma\in F\},
  1513: \]
  1514: where \(\rho_*(\widetilde\sigma_N)\) is computed from the projected root gap
  1515: law, not from the raw empirical intensity
  1516: \(\widehat r_{0,N}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\).  The associated finite-window word
  1517: probabilities are then the renewal inclusion products and finite
  1518: inclusion--exclusion probabilities of this lifted projected law.
  1519: If any denominator, projection-root, interior-root, or declared numerical
  1520: certificate required by the operational protocol fails, no projected plug-in
  1521: word probabilities are formed.  The output is a projected plug-in word-law
  1522: reconstruction only: it gives projected word probabilities as a
  1523: renewal-consistent plug-in law, not confidence intervals, bands, \(p\)-values,
  1524: or coverage statements for those word probabilities.
  1525: Algorithm~4 in \Cref{app:finite-record-implementation-protocols-main} gives the finite-window
  1526: coefficient recursion, truncation convention, scalar-mean tail certificate,
  1527: and withholding rule used for numerical reconstruction.
  1528: \end{definition}
  1529: 
  1530: \begin{proposition}[Population complete-law reconstruction from three inclusions]\label{lem:algorithmic-complete-law-reconstruction-main}
  1531: Work in the stable quotient domain of
  1532: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}.  The following deterministic
  1533: map reconstructs the complete stationary visible renewal law from the
  1534: population three-inclusion coordinates
  1535: \[
  1536: r=(r_0,r_1,r_2)
  1537: =\bigl(\PP(A_0=1),\PP(A_0=A_1=1),\PP(A_0=A_2=1)\bigr).
  1538: \]
  1539: It is a population reconstruction theorem only; the finite-record projection
  1540: theorem is \Cref{prop:finite-record-complete-law-renewal-consistency-main},
  1541: and numerical truncation guidance is isolated in
  1542: \Cref{rem:complete-law-numerical-implementation-main}.
  1543: 
  1544: \begin{enumerate}
  1545: \item Start from the population three inclusion coordinates
  1546: \[
  1547: r_0=\PP(A_0=1),\qquad
  1548: r_1=\PP(A_0=A_1=1),\qquad
  1549: r_2=\PP(A_0=A_2=1),
  1550: \]
  1551: and retain the triple only when
  1552: \[
  1553: r_0>0,\qquad \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}:=r_1-r_0^2\ne0 .
  1554: \]
  1555: 
  1556: \item Compute the adjacent Palm mass and hidden-tail ratio
  1557: \[
  1558: a=\frac{r_1}{r_0},\qquad
  1559: \lambda=\frac{r_2-r_0^2}{r_1-r_0^2}.
  1560: \]
  1561: Then form the symmetric root coordinates
  1562: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-sigma-main}
  1563: \sigma_1=1-a+\lambda,\qquad
  1564: \sigma_2=r_0(1-\lambda)-a+\lambda .
  1565: \end{equation}
  1566: For the constrained D-MAP population,
  1567: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality} gives
  1568: \[
  1569: (\sigma_1,\sigma_2)=(p+s,ps).
  1570: \]
  1571: 
  1572: \item Recover the unordered survival-factor pair
  1573: \[
  1574: \{z_1,z_2\}
  1575: =\{ \text{roots of } z^2-\sigma_1z+\sigma_2=0\}.
  1576: \]
  1577: Only this unordered pair is a visible-law object.  At the population
  1578: constrained D-MAP point, \(\sigma\) lies in the feasible set \(F\) equivalently
  1579: characterized in \eqref{eq:feasible-symmetric-coordinate-set-main}.
  1580: 
  1581: \item Recover the Palm zero-run gap law.  Put
  1582: \(\theta_i=-\log z_i\).  If \(z_1\ne z_2\), set
  1583: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-gap-main}
  1584: g_k=
  1585: \frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)z_1^k-\theta_1(1-z_2)z_2^k}
  1586:      {\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1587: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1588: \end{equation}
  1589: If \(z_1=z_2=z\), put \(\theta=-\log z\) and use the continuous-limit law
  1590: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-gap-diagonal-main}
  1591: g_k=z^k\bigl((1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\bigr),
  1592: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1593: \end{equation}
  1594: For population roots this is the D-MAP Palm gap law.  For any later
  1595: finite-record projected root pair retained in \((0,1)^2\), the same formulas
  1596: are certified as a nonnegative normalized gap law by
  1597: \Cref{lem:projected-root-gap-law-valid-main}, independently of the noisy
  1598: empirical intensity used to construct the projected coordinate.
  1599: 
  1600: \item Compute the Palm renewal masses
  1601: \[
  1602: u_0=1,\qquad
  1603: u_n=\PP(n\in\varXi\mid0\in\varXi)
  1604:     =\sum_{j=1}^{n}\!
  1605:       \sum_{\substack{k_1,\ldots,k_j\ge0\\
  1606:       (k_1+1)+\cdots+(k_j+1)=n}}
  1607:       g_{k_1}\cdots g_{k_j},
  1608: \qquad n\ge1 .
  1609: \]
  1610: Equivalently, if \(G\) has law \(g\), then
  1611: \[
  1612: u_n=\sum_{j\ge1}\PP\bigl((G_1+1)+\cdots+(G_j+1)=n\bigr),
  1613: \]
  1614: where the sum is finite because each cycle length \(G_i+1\) is at least
  1615: \(1\).
  1616: Equivalently, with the cycle generating function
  1617: \[
  1618: H(t)=\sum_{k\ge0}g_k t^{k+1},
  1619: \]
  1620: the Palm renewal generating function is
  1621: \begin{equation}\label{eq:renewal-generating-function-complete-law-main}
  1622: U(t):=\sum_{n\ge0}u_n t^n
  1623: =\frac{1}{1-H(t)}
  1624: =\frac{1}{1-\sum_{k\ge0}g_k t^{k+1}} .
  1625: \end{equation}
  1626: Equivalently, the coefficients satisfy the finite recursion
  1627: \begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-window-renewal-recursion-main}
  1628: u_0=1,\qquad
  1629: u_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}g_{\ell-1}u_{n-\ell},
  1630: \qquad n\ge1 .
  1631: \end{equation}
  1632: 
  1633: \item Compute the stationary renewal intensity from the same gap law,
  1634: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-rho-star-main}
  1635: \rho_*(\sigma)
  1636: =\frac{1}{\EE(G+1)}
  1637: =\left(1+\sum_{k\ge0}k\,g_k\right)^{-1}.
  1638: \end{equation}
  1639: At the population input produced by the constrained D-MAP, this value equals
  1640: the original inclusion coordinate \(r_0\).  This equality is a conclusion of
  1641: the population model, not a license to combine a noisy finite-record
  1642: \(\widehat r_0\) with a separately projected root pair.
  1643: 
  1644: \item Apply the renewal inclusion product and then finite
  1645: inclusion--exclusion.  For every nonempty
  1646: \(B=\{i_1<\cdots<i_m\}\),
  1647: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-inclusion-main}
  1648: \PP(B\subset\varXi)
  1649: =\rho_*(\sigma)\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}u_{i_{\ell+1}-i_\ell},
  1650: \end{equation}
  1651: with the empty-set inclusion probability equal to \(1\).  For every finite
  1652: window \(I\) and word \(S\subset I\),
  1653: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-word-main}
  1654: \PP(\varXi\cap I=S)
  1655: =\sum_{C\subset I\setminus S}(-1)^{|C|}
  1656: \PP(S\cup C\subset\varXi).
  1657: \end{equation}
  1658: \end{enumerate}
  1659: 
  1660: At the population constrained D-MAP point the procedure is exact and
  1661: determines the complete finite-dimensional visible law on the exchange
  1662: quotient.  No step assigns the two roots to labelled attempt/recovery rates;
  1663: that additional ordering is precisely the branch-map convention of
  1664: \Cref{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}.
  1665: \end{proposition}
  1666: 
  1667: \begin{proof}
  1668: The domain test permits the scalar divisions in Step~2.  The formulas for
  1669: \(a\) and \(\lambda\) are those in
  1670: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}, and
  1671: \Cref{lem:quotient-inverse-symmetric-algebra-main} identifies
  1672: \eqref{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-sigma-main} with \((p+s,ps)\) at the
  1673: constrained D-MAP population point.  Thus the roots in Step~3 are the
  1674: unordered pair \(\{p,s\}\), with no labelled branch information.
  1675: 
  1676: Step~4 is exactly \Cref{lem:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-main}: the
  1677: unequal-root formula is the symmetric dimensionless gap law
  1678: \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}, and the equal-root formula is its continuous
  1679: limit.  Given this gap law, the visible process is the stationary renewal
  1680: process supplied by \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main}.  Step~5 is the renewal
  1681: equation for Palm return probabilities, written first as a finite convolution
  1682: sum at fixed time \(n\) and equivalently as the generating-function identity
  1683: \eqref{eq:renewal-generating-function-complete-law-main}.  Comparing
  1684: coefficients in \(U(t)(1-H(t))=1\) gives
  1685: \eqref{eq:finite-window-renewal-recursion-main}.
  1686: The stationary renewal intensity in Step~6 is \(1/\EE(G+1)\); for the
  1687: constrained D-MAP population this equals
  1688: \(\PP(A_0=1)=r_0\) by stationarity of the renewal process already identified
  1689: in \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main}.  Finally, Step~7 is
  1690: \Cref{cor:renewal-principal-minor-word-law-main}: first multiply Palm renewal
  1691: masses to obtain marked-set inclusions, then use finite inclusion--exclusion
  1692: over the unprescribed sites.  These operations determine every finite word
  1693: probability and therefore the complete stationary visible law.
  1694: \end{proof}
  1695: 
  1696: \begin{remark}[Numerical note for complete-law finite-window evaluation]\label{rem:complete-law-numerical-implementation-main}
  1697: This note concerns only the numerical evaluation of the population law in
  1698: \Cref{lem:algorithmic-complete-law-reconstruction-main} or of a retained
  1699: projected finite-record law in
  1700: \Cref{prop:finite-record-complete-law-renewal-consistency-main}; it is not an
  1701: additional statistical assertion.  For a finite window \(I\), put \(d_I=0\) if
  1702: \(|I|\le1\), and otherwise put \(d_I=\max I-\min I\).  The inclusion products
  1703: need only \(u_0,\ldots,u_{d_I}\).  If an implementation forms the truncated
  1704: cycle polynomial
  1705: \[
  1706: H_K(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}g_k t^{k+1},
  1707: \]
  1708: then every coefficient \(u_n\) with \(0\le n\le d_I\) is exactly unchanged
  1709: whenever \(K\ge d_I-1\).  Thus renewal-mass truncation contributes no error to
  1710: word probabilities on \(I\) once the gap law is kept through \(g_{d_I-1}\).
  1711: 
  1712: Only the scalar mean in \(\rho_*(\sigma)\) may require an infinite-tail
  1713: evaluation.  It may be computed from the closed root sums
  1714: \[
  1715: \sum_{k\ge0}k g_k
  1716: =
  1717: \begin{cases}
  1718: \displaystyle
  1719: \frac{\theta_2 z_1/(1-z_1)-\theta_1 z_2/(1-z_2)}
  1720:      {\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1721: & z_1\ne z_2,\\[1.2em]
  1722: \displaystyle
  1723: \frac{z}{1-z}+\frac{\theta z}{(1-z)^2},
  1724: & z_1=z_2=z,\ \theta=-\log z,
  1725: \end{cases}
  1726: \]
  1727: or from a truncated sum with a reported tail certificate.  For any displayed
  1728: \(\bar r\) with \(\max(z_1,z_2)\le \bar r<1\), set
  1729: \[
  1730: C_{\bar r}=
  1731: \frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)+\theta_1(1-z_2)}
  1732:      {|\theta_2-\theta_1|}
  1733: \]
  1734: in the unequal-root case, and set
  1735: \[
  1736: C_{\bar r}=(1-z)+\theta,\qquad \theta=-\log z
  1737: \]
  1738: in the diagonal case.  Then
  1739: \[
  1740: 0\le g_k\le C_{\bar r}(1+k)\bar r^k,\qquad k\ge0 .
  1741: \]
  1742: For
  1743: \[
  1744: m_K=1+\sum_{k=0}^{K}k g_k,\qquad
  1745: \varepsilon_K=C_{\bar r}\sum_{k>K}k(1+k)\bar r^k ,
  1746: \]
  1747: one has \(0\le\EE(G+1)-m_K\le\varepsilon_K\).  If
  1748: \(\varepsilon_K<m_K\), the intensity \(\rho_K=1/m_K\) computed from the
  1749: truncated mean satisfies
  1750: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rho-truncation-error-bound-main}
  1751: |\rho_K-\rho_*(\sigma)|
  1752: \le \frac{\varepsilon_K}{m_K(m_K-\varepsilon_K)} .
  1753: \end{equation}
  1754: Since every renewal mass used in the inclusion product is a probability, this
  1755: contributes at most
  1756: \[
  1757: 2^{|I|}\frac{\varepsilon_K}{m_K(m_K-\varepsilon_K)}
  1758: \]
  1759: to any word probability on \(I\) after finite inclusion--exclusion.
  1760: \end{remark}
  1761: 
  1762: \begin{proposition}[Separated-root labelled chart]\label{prop:population-separated-root-labelled-chart-main}
  1763: In the setting of \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}, suppose that the
  1764: quotient inverse gives
  1765: \(\sigma=(\sigma_1,\sigma_2)=(p+s,ps)\) with
  1766: \[
  1767: \Delta_\sigma=\sigma_1^2-4\sigma_2>0 .
  1768: \]
  1769: Then the two roots of \(z^2-\sigma_1z+\sigma_2=0\) are distinct elements of
  1770: \((0,1)\).  For a known design interval \(\Delta\tau\), and under the
  1771: branch-map convention of
  1772: \Cref{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}, a supplied branch map
  1773: \(\mathcal B\) orders those roots as \((p_{\mathcal B},s_{\mathcal B})\), and
  1774: on that chart
  1775: \[
  1776: (\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})
  1777: =\left(-\frac{\log p_{\mathcal B}}{\Delta\tau},
  1778:        -\frac{\log s_{\mathcal B}}{\Delta\tau}\right).
  1779: \]
  1780: If \(\Delta_\sigma=0\), the separated labelled chart is not defined; the
  1781: quotient still identifies the common root and hence the common dimensionless
  1782: product, with the common numerical rate available only when \(\Delta\tau\) is
  1783: known, as in \Cref{lem:diagonal-common-rate-main}.
  1784: \end{proposition}
  1785: 
  1786: \begin{proof}
  1787: The roots of \(z^2-\sigma_1z+\sigma_2=0\) are the unordered pair
  1788: \(\{p,s\}\) by \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}.  The strict
  1789: discriminant condition is exactly \(p\ne s\), so the two roots can be
  1790: separated as numerical values.  The exchange symmetry proved in
  1791: \Cref{lem:gap-law-exchange-symmetry-main} shows that the visible stationary
  1792: law is unchanged by swapping the two roots.  Therefore the assignment of one
  1793: root to the attempt branch and the other to the recovery branch cannot be
  1794: inferred from the visible law and must be supplied externally.  Applying
  1795: \(z\mapsto-\log z/\Delta\tau\) to the two externally ordered roots gives the
  1796: displayed labelled rates for the known design interval.  At
  1797: \(\Delta_\sigma=0\) the two roots coalesce, and the denominator of the
  1798: separated-root differential vanishes; only the quotient common-root statement,
  1799: or the common-rate statement when \(\Delta\tau\) is known, remains.
  1800: \end{proof}
  1801: 
  1802: \begin{lemma}[Gap law from the quotient root pair]\label{lem:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-main}
  1803: Let \((\sigma_1,\sigma_2)\) be the symmetric coordinates recovered in
  1804: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}, and let
  1805: \(\{z_1,z_2\}\subset(0,1)\) be the unordered roots of
  1806: \[
  1807: z^2-\sigma_1 z+\sigma_2=0 .
  1808: \]
  1809: Put \(\theta_i=-\log z_i\).  If \(z_1\ne z_2\), then the Palm zero-run law is
  1810: the symmetric expression
  1811: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-main}
  1812: g_k=
  1813: \frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)z_1^k-\theta_1(1-z_2)z_2^k}
  1814:      {\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1815: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1816: \end{equation}
  1817: The right-hand side is unchanged by exchanging \(z_1\) and \(z_2\).  On the
  1818: diagonal \(\sigma=(2z,z^2)\), \(0<z<1\), where
  1819: \(\theta=-\log z\), the same quotient coordinate gives the continuous-limit
  1820: law
  1821: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-diagonal-main}
  1822: g_k=z^k\bigl((1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\bigr),
  1823: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1824: \end{equation}
  1825: Consequently the recovered unordered root pair determines the complete renewal
  1826: gap law, including at a double root.
  1827: \end{lemma}
  1828: 
  1829: \begin{proof}
  1830: For distinct roots choose an arbitrary ordering and write
  1831: \[
  1832: p=z_1,\qquad s=z_2,\qquad
  1833: x=-\log p=\theta_1,\qquad y=-\log s=\theta_2 .
  1834: \]
  1835: Substitution in the dimensionless form
  1836: \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main} gives
  1837: \eqref{eq:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-main}.  If the two roots are exchanged,
  1838: both the numerator and the denominator in
  1839: \eqref{eq:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-main} change sign, so the value of
  1840: \(g_k\) is an unordered-root function.  At a double root \(z_1=z_2=z\),
  1841: \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main} reads the unequal-root expression by the continuous
  1842: equal-rate dictionary \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}; with
  1843: \(\theta=-\log z\), this is exactly
  1844: \eqref{eq:gap-law-from-quotient-roots-diagonal-main}.
  1845: \end{proof}
  1846: 
  1847: \begin{lemma}[Projected quotient roots define a stochastic gap law]\label{lem:projected-root-gap-law-valid-main}
  1848: Let \(z_1,z_2\in(0,1)\) and put \(\theta_i=-\log z_i\).  If
  1849: \(z_1\ne z_2\), define
  1850: \[
  1851: g_k=
  1852: \frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)z_1^k-\theta_1(1-z_2)z_2^k}
  1853:      {\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1854: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1855: \]
  1856: If \(z_1=z_2=z\), put \(\theta=-\log z\) and define
  1857: \[
  1858: g_k=z^k\{(1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\},
  1859: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1860: \]
  1861: Then \(g_k\ge0\) for every \(k\ge0\) and
  1862: \[
  1863: \sum_{k\ge0}g_k=1 .
  1864: \]
  1865: More explicitly, the displayed \(g_k\) is the \(D\)-row path probability
  1866: \[
  1867: g_k=e_D^\top T_0(z_1,z_2)^kT_1(z_1,z_2)\mathbf1
  1868: \]
  1869: of a two-state stochastic triangular labelled kernel.  Thus every finite
  1870: projected root pair retained in the interior of \(F\) produces an honest
  1871: nonnegative normalized Palm zero-run law before any finite-window
  1872: probabilities are formed.
  1873: \end{lemma}
  1874: 
  1875: \begin{proof}
  1876: First suppose \(z_1\ne z_2\).  Define
  1877: \[
  1878: b=\frac{\theta_2(z_1-z_2)}{\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1879: \qquad
  1880: a=\frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)-\theta_1(1-z_2)}
  1881:         {\theta_2-\theta_1}.
  1882: \]
  1883: These coefficients are nonnegative.  Indeed, after choosing the order
  1884: \(z_1=e^{-\theta_1}\), \(z_2=e^{-\theta_2}\), they have the integral
  1885: representations
  1886: \[
  1887: b=\int_0^1\theta_2 e^{-\theta_2 r}e^{-\theta_1(1-r)}\,dr,
  1888: \qquad
  1889: a=\int_0^1\theta_2 e^{-\theta_2 r}
  1890:        \{1-e^{-\theta_1(1-r)}\}\,dr ,
  1891: \]
  1892: with the displayed quotient values obtained by evaluating the elementary
  1893: integrals.  Moreover \(a+b=1-z_2\).  Hence
  1894: \[
  1895: T_0(z_1,z_2)=
  1896: \begin{pmatrix}
  1897: z_1&0\\ b&z_2
  1898: \end{pmatrix},
  1899: \qquad
  1900: T_1(z_1,z_2)=

 succeeded in 357ms:
     1: \section{Record, inverse, and finite-record results}\label{sec:records}
     2: 
     3: This section gives the record-side results in compressed form.  Each main
     4: result is read under the constrained D-MAP record assumption
     5: \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}, with any extra regularity or
     6: finite-record protocol stated locally.  Between those statements, all
     7: probabilities below are probabilities of the stationary visible binary record
     8: generated by the sampled-counter labelled kernels \(T_0,T_1\).
     9: The killed entries \(T_1(R,R)=T_1(D,R)=0\) are used as assumptions, not as
    10: conclusions; \Cref{prop:model-fragility-boundary-reset-main} states exactly
    11: which renewal and inverse conclusions are lost for same-bin recovery leakage.
    12: Throughout this section \((A_n)_{n\in\mathbb Z}\) denotes that two-sided
    13: stationary extension.
    14: One-sided strings such as \(A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1}\) are finite restrictions used
    15: only for observed-record statistics; when a proof conditions on a past event
    16: or shifts a renewal epoch, it is using the same stationary two-sided process.
    17: 
    18: \subsection{Model and renewal law}
    19: 
    20: \begin{proposition}[Constrained D-MAP labelled kernels]\label{prop:instrument-kernels-main}
    21: Fix \(\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau>0\).  With hidden states ordered as
    22: \((R,D)\), let
    23: \begin{equation}\label{eq:t0-t1-main}
    24: T_0=
    25: \begin{pmatrix}
    26: p&0\\ b&s
    27: \end{pmatrix},
    28: \qquad
    29: T_1=
    30: \begin{pmatrix}
    31: 0&1-p\\0&a
    32: \end{pmatrix},
    33: \end{equation}
    34: where
    35: \begin{equation}\label{eq:p-c-main}
    36: p=e^{-\Gamma\Delta\tau},
    37: \qquad
    38: s=e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau},
    39: \end{equation}
    40: and
    41: \begin{equation}\label{eq:a-b-main}
    42: a=\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}
    43: \bigl(1-e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}\bigr)\,dr,
    44: \qquad
    45: b=\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}
    46: e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}\,dr .
    47: \end{equation}
    48: For \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\ne\Gamma\),
    49: \[
    50: a=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)-\Gamma(1-s)}
    51:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
    52: \qquad
    53: b=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(p-s)}
    54:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
    55: \]
    56: with continuous equal-rate limits.  The labelled-kernel convention is
    57: \((T_i)_{XY}=\PP(A_n=i,H_{n+1}=Y\mid H_n=X)\), and word probabilities are
    58: \(\pi T_{u_0}\cdots T_{u_{m-1}}\mathbf1\) from the stationary hidden law.
    59: These are the kernels fixed in \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}.
    60: \end{proposition}
    61: 
    62: \begin{proof}
    63: The two rows define a labelled transition kernel directly.  From state \(R\),
    64: the zero-labelled transition remains in \(R\) with probability \(p\), while the
    65: one-labelled transition goes to \(D\) with probability \(1-p\).  From state
    66: \(D\), the zero-labelled transition has weights \(b\) to \(R\) and \(s\) to
    67: \(D\), and the one-labelled transition has weight \(a\) to \(D\).  The
    68: integrals define nonnegative weights with \(a+b=1-s\), so the two row sums of
    69: \(T_0+T_1\) are one.  Thus \(T_0,T_1\) form the stated D-MAP kernels.  The
    70: displayed unequal-rate formulas are the elementary evaluations of the two
    71: integrals, with continuous limits on the equal-rate diagonal.
    72: \end{proof}
    73: 
    74: \begin{lemma}[Equal-rate and critical notation]\label{lem:equal-rate-critical-convention-main}
    75: The survival factors are always
    76: \[
    77: p=e^{-\Gamma\Delta\tau},\qquad
    78: s=e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau},
    79: \]
    80: and throughout the stipulated-kernel algebra
    81: \[
    82: q=1-p .
    83: \]
    84: The hidden eigenvalue is
    85: \begin{equation}\label{eq:lambda-hid-main}
    86: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}
    87: =p-b
    88: =\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}s-\Gamma p}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
    89: \quad(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\ne\Gamma),
    90: \end{equation}
    91: with continuous equal-rate value
    92: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=e^{-y}(1-y)\), where
    93: \(y=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau=\Gamma\Delta\tau\) on the equal-rate
    94: diagonal.  Put
    95: \[
    96: x=\Gamma\Delta\tau,\qquad y=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau .
    97: \]
    98: For \(x\ne y\),
    99: \[
   100: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0
   101: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   102: x e^{-x}=y e^{-y}.
   103: \]
   104: The equation \(x e^{-x}=y e^{-y}\) always has the formal solution \(x=y\).
   105: For \(y\ne1\) this diagonal solution is only the removable zero of the
   106: multiplied quotient numerator, not a zero of the continuous eigenvalue
   107: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  The critical rate is therefore the non-removable
   108: branch
   109: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma-crit-piecewise-main}
   110: \Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau)
   111: =\Delta\tau^{-1}
   112: \begin{cases}
   113: x_+(y),&0<y<1,\quad x_+(y)>1,\quad x_+(y)e^{-x_+(y)}=y e^{-y},\\
   114: 1,&y=1,\\
   115: x_-(y),&y>1,\quad 0<x_-(y)<1,\quad x_-(y)e^{-x_-(y)}=y e^{-y}.
   116: \end{cases}
   117: \end{equation}
   118: Equivalently, for \(y\ne1\) it is the unique positive solution of
   119: \(x e^{-x}=y e^{-y}\) on the opposite side of \(1\) from \(y\), and the
   120: removable equal-rate branch \(x=y\) is explicitly excluded.  Only at \(y=1\)
   121: does the equal-rate branch become a genuine zero, giving
   122: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=\kappa_{\mathrm r}=1/\Delta\tau\).
   123: \end{lemma}
   124: 
   125: \begin{proof}
   126: The identity \(p-b\) and the quotient form follow from
   127: \eqref{eq:a-b-main}.  The equal-rate value is l'Hopital's rule, equivalently
   128: \(b=y e^{-y}\) when \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}=\Gamma\), and hence
   129: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=e^{-y}(1-y)\).  For \(x\ne y\), multiplying the
   130: quotient numerator by \(\Delta\tau\) gives the scalar equation
   131: \(x e^{-x}=y e^{-y}\).  The function \(x\mapsto xe^{-x}\) is strictly
   132: increasing on \((0,1)\), strictly decreasing on \((1,\infty)\), and has its
   133: only maximum at \(1\).  Hence the level through \(y\ne1\) has exactly one
   134: positive solution on the opposite side of \(1\), in addition to the removable
   135: solution \(x=y\).  This gives the two non-removable branches in
   136: \eqref{eq:gamma-crit-piecewise-main}.  When \(y=1\), the opposite branch
   137: coalesces with the diagonal and the equal-rate limit \(e^{-1}(1-1)=0\) gives
   138: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=1/\Delta\tau=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\).
   139: \end{proof}
   140: 
   141: For later audits, the equal-rate diagonal
   142: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), \(y=\Gamma\Delta\tau\), \(p=e^{-y}\), is
   143: summarized by the single dictionary
   144: \begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}
   145: \begin{aligned}
   146: a&=1-(1+y)p,&
   147: b&=yp,\\
   148: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}&=p-b=(1-y)p,&
   149: S_k&=\PP(G\ge k)=p^k(1+yk),\\
   150: g_k&=p^k\bigl((1-p)(1+yk)-yp\bigr),&
   151: h_k&=\frac{(1-p)+y(k(1-p)-p)}{1+yk},
   152: \qquad k\ge0 .
   153: \end{aligned}
   154: \end{equation}
   155: 
   156: \begin{lemma}[Regeneration and zero-run law]\label{lem:renewal-gap-main}
   157: Let \(G\) be the number of zeros between consecutive clicks in the stationary
   158: constrained D-MAP record.  Click times are regeneration times.  For
   159: \(k\ge0\),
   160: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-main}
   161: g_k=\PP(G=k)=e_D^\top T_0^kT_1\mathbf1 .
   162: \end{equation}
   163: If \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\ne\Gamma\), then
   164: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-closed-main}
   165: g_k=
   166: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   167:      {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   168: \end{equation}
   169: The gap law has full support on \(\mathbb N_0\) and an exponential tail.
   170: \end{lemma}
   171: 
   172: \begin{proof}
   173: After a click the next hidden state is \(D\), independently of the earlier
   174: record.  Thus a gap of \(k\) zeros followed by a click has probability
   175: \eqref{eq:gap-main}.  Since \(T_0\) is lower triangular,
   176: \[
   177: \textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}=0\quad(k=0),
   178: \]
   179: with the displayed sum interpreted as empty when \(k=0\), and
   180: \[
   181: T_0^k=
   182: \begin{pmatrix}
   183: p^k&0\\
   184: b\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}&s^k
   185: \end{pmatrix}.
   186: \]
   187: Therefore
   188: \[
   189: e_D^\top T_0^kT_1\mathbf1
   190: =b(1-p)\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}+as^k,
   191: \]
   192: where the comma only separates the display from the following substitution.
   193: For \(p\ne s\),
   194: \[
   195: \sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}=\frac{p^k-s^k}{p-s}.
   196: \]
   197: Substituting
   198: \[
   199: a=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)-\Gamma(1-s)}
   200:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   201: \qquad
   202: b=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(p-s)}
   203:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
   204: \]
   205: therefore gives
   206: \[
   207: b(1-p)\frac{p^k-s^k}{p-s}+as^k
   208: =
   209: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   210:      {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   211: \]
   212: which is \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main}.  If \(p=s\), equivalently
   213: \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}=\Gamma\), the same identity is read by the continuous
   214: equal-rate dictionary \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}.  All terms are
   215: probabilities of disjoint paths, so they are positive, and the two survival
   216: factors lie in \((0,1)\).
   217: \end{proof}
   218: 
   219: \begin{corollary}[Support of finite binary words]\label{cor:word-support-main}
   220: Every finite binary word has positive probability under the stationary
   221: constrained D-MAP record.
   222: \end{corollary}
   223: 
   224: \begin{proof}
   225: Let the word be \(w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-1})\), with \(m\ge1\), and write
   226: \[
   227: S=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}:w_i=1\}.
   228: \]
   229: We work in the two-sided stationary renewal click set
   230: \(\varXi=\{n:A_n=1\}\).  Embed the observed window in the longer interval
   231: \([-1,m]\) and force clicks at both artificial endpoints.  More explicitly,
   232: list the finite set
   233: \[
   234: C=\{-1\}\cup S\cup\{m\}=\{c_0<c_1<\cdots<c_J\}.
   235: \]
   236: Consider the event that \(c_0,c_1,\ldots,c_J\) are consecutive renewal
   237: clicks, with no further clicks between them.  Under the stationary law this
   238: event has probability
   239: \[
   240: \PP(c_0\in\varXi)\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}
   241: \PP\bigl(G=c_{j+1}-c_j-1\bigr)
   242: =\rho\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}g_{c_{j+1}-c_j-1}.
   243: \]
   244: Each displayed gap length is a nonnegative integer, including at the two
   245: observed-window boundaries.  By \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main},
   246: \(\rho>0\) and \(g_k>0\) for every \(k\ge0\), so the event has positive
   247: probability.  On this event the restriction of the click set to
   248: \(\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\) is exactly \(S\): the forced interior clicks are precisely
   249: the one-sites of \(w\), and the ``consecutive clicks'' condition rules out
   250: clicks at every zero-site.  Hence the cylinder probability of \(w\) is at
   251: least this positive probability.  The empty word has probability \(1\).
   252: \end{proof}
   253: 
   254: \subsection{Exact dependence, hazard, and count diagnostics}
   255: 
   256: These are falsification diagnostics only; no size, power, or post-selection
   257: coverage theorem is proved for these diagnostics.
   258: The hazard, Fano, and low-lag formulas in this subsection have the inferential
   259: status fixed in \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}.  The hazard,
   260: spectral-density, and Fano displays in this subsection are population
   261: identities or inputs to later sandwich/projection procedures.
   262: 
   263: \begin{lemma}[Stationary hidden law]\label{lem:stationary-main}
   264: For \(P=T_0+T_1=\begin{psmallmatrix}p&q\\ b&1-b\end{psmallmatrix}\), the
   265: stationary hidden law is
   266: \[
   267: \pi=\left(\frac b{q+b},\frac{q}{q+b}\right).
   268: \]
   269: With \(q=1-p\) as fixed in \Cref{lem:equal-rate-critical-convention-main}, the
   270: stationary click density is
   271: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rho-main}
   272: \rho=\pi T_1\mathbf1=\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}.
   273: \end{equation}
   274: Moreover \(a<q(1-s)<q\), and therefore
   275: \(a-\rho=-b(q-a)/(q+b)<0\).
   276: \end{lemma}
   277: 
   278: \begin{proof}
   279: Solving \(\pi P=\pi\) gives the displayed law.  Since
   280: \(u=T_1\mathbf1=(q,a)^\top\) and \(a+b=1-s\), \(\rho=\pi u\) gives
   281: \eqref{eq:rho-main}.  For \(r>0\),
   282: \[
   283: 1-e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}<1-e^{-\Gamma\Delta\tau}=q,
   284: \]
   285: so the defining integral for \(a\) gives
   286: \[
   287: a<q\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}\,dr
   288: =q(1-s)<q .
   289: \]
   290: The sign follows from
   291: \[
   292: a-\rho=-\frac{b(q-a)}{q+b}
   293: \]
   294: and the strict positivity of \(b\) and \(q-a\).
   295: \end{proof}
   296: 
   297: \begin{lemma}[Covariance transfer through the hidden mode]\label{lem:covariance-transfer-main}
   298: For the stationary D-MAP record,
   299: \begin{equation}\label{eq:cov-transfer-main}
   300: \PP(A_n=1\mid A_0=1)
   301: =\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1},
   302: \qquad
   303: \operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_n)=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}
   304: \end{equation}
   305: for \(n\ge1\).
   306: \end{lemma}
   307: 
   308: \begin{proof}
   309: With \(q=1-p\) and \(u=(q,a)^\top\),
   310: \[
   311: u-\rho\mathbf1=\frac{q-a}{q+b}\binom {q}{-b},
   312: \qquad
   313: P\binom {q}{-b}=(p-b)\binom {q}{-b}.
   314: \]
   315: Thus
   316: \[
   317: P^{n-1}u=\rho\mathbf1+
   318: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{n-1}(u-\rho\mathbf1).
   319: \]
   320: A click at time \(0\) sets the next hidden state to \(D\).  Hence
   321: \[
   322: \PP(A_n=1\mid A_0=1)
   323: =e_D^\top P^{n-1}u
   324: =\rho+\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{n-1}(a-\rho),
   325: \]
   326: because the \(D\)-component of \(u-\rho\mathbf1\) is \(a-\rho\).  Multiplying
   327: by \(\rho=\PP(A_0=1)\) and subtracting \(\rho^2\) gives the covariance in
   328: \eqref{eq:cov-transfer-main}.
   329: \end{proof}
   330: 
   331: \begin{lemma}[Critical-rate uniqueness and covariance phase]\label{lem:critical-rate-main}
   332: For fixed \(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau>0\), the equation
   333: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}(\Gamma)=0\) has the unique positive solution
   334: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau)\) defined by the
   335: piecewise non-removable branch
   336: \eqref{eq:gamma-crit-piecewise-main}.  When
   337: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}\ne\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), this is equivalently
   338: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma-c-main}
   339: \Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}\Delta\tau}
   340: =\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau}.
   341: \end{equation}
   342: The displayed scalar equation also has the diagonal solution
   343: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), but that solution is removable
   344: for \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\) unless
   345: \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau=1\).  Thus on the diagonal
   346: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), the zero occurs only at
   347: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}=1/\Delta\tau\).
   348: The sign of \(\operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_n)\) is the sign of
   349: \(-\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}\).
   350: \end{lemma}
   351: 
   352: \begin{proof}
   353: The uniqueness is the branch statement in
   354: \Cref{lem:equal-rate-critical-convention-main}.  The covariance sign follows
   355: from \(\rho>0\) and \(a-\rho<0\) in \Cref{lem:stationary-main}.
   356: \end{proof}
   357: 
   358: \begin{lemma}[Hidden-mode contraction]\label{lem:hidden-mode-contraction-main}
   359: For every positive constrained D-MAP setting,
   360: \[
   361: |\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1 .
   362: \]
   363: In particular the shifted geometric Fourier series in
   364: \Cref{prop:spectrum-main} is absolutely convergent.
   365: \end{lemma}
   366: 
   367: \begin{proof}
   368: The transition matrix
   369: \[
   370: P=T_0+T_1=
   371: \begin{pmatrix}
   372: p&1-p\\ b&1-b
   373: \end{pmatrix}
   374: \]
   375: is a two-state stochastic matrix with strictly positive off-diagonal entries
   376: \(1-p>0\) and \(b>0\).  Its eigenvalues are \(1\) and
   377: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\).  The upper bound is immediate from \(p<1\)
   378: and \(b>0\): \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}<1\).  For the lower bound, \(p>0\) and
   379: \[
   380: b=\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}
   381: e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}\,dr
   382: <\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}\,dr
   383: =1-s<1 .
   384: \]
   385: Thus \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b>-1\).  Hence
   386: \(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1\).  Absolute convergence follows from
   387: \(\sum_{n\ge1}|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|^{n-1}<\infty\).
   388: \end{proof}
   389: 
   390: \begin{lemma}[Finite hidden-start path-space contraction]\label{lem:finite-hidden-start-path-contraction-main}
   391: Fix one positive constrained D-MAP setting and put \(P=T_0+T_1\).  Let
   392: \(\pi\) be the stationary hidden law.  For a hidden initial law \(\nu\), let
   393: \(\PP_\nu\) denote the one-sided hidden/visible law started from \(H_0\sim\nu\),
   394: and let \(\PP_\pi\) denote the stationary-start law.  There are constants
   395: \(C<\infty\) and \(\theta\in(0,1)\), depending only on the fixed setting, such
   396: that for every burn-in length \(L\ge0\), every retained length \(N\ge1\), and
   397: every set \(B\subset\{0,1\}^N\),
   398: \begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-hidden-start-path-tv-main}
   399: \left|
   400: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   401: -
   402: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   403: \right|
   404: \le C\theta^L .
   405: \end{equation}
   406: Equivalently,
   407: \[
   408: \sup_{B\subset\{0,1\}^N}
   409: \left|
   410: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   411: -
   412: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   413: \right|
   414: \le C\theta^L ,
   415: \]
   416: up to the harmless convention-dependent factor in the definition of total
   417: variation.  The bound is uniform in \(N\).  Consequently, if \(L_N\to\infty\)
   418: and \(S_N\) is any measurable statistic of the retained visible word
   419: \((A_{L_N},\ldots,A_{L_N+N-1})\), then every stationary-start convergence in
   420: distribution, convergence in probability, or event-probability limit for
   421: \(S_N\) transfers unchanged to the finite hidden start.
   422: \end{lemma}
   423: 
   424: \begin{proof}
   425: For a word \(w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{N-1})\), write
   426: \(T_w=T_{w_0}\cdots T_{w_{N-1}}\).  For a set
   427: \(B\subset\{0,1\}^N\), put \(M_B=\sum_{w\in B}T_w\).  Since
   428: \(\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^N}T_w=P^N\), the matrix \(M_B\) is substochastic: its
   429: entries are nonnegative and \(0\le M_B\mathbf1\le\mathbf1\) coordinatewise.
   430: The retained visible word probability from the finite start is therefore
   431: \[
   432: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   433: =\nu P^L M_B\mathbf1,
   434: \]
   435: while stationarity gives
   436: \[
   437: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   438: =\pi M_B\mathbf1 .
   439: \]
   440: Thus the difference is
   441: \[
   442: (\nu P^L-\pi)M_B\mathbf1 .
   443: \]
   444: Because \(0\le M_B\mathbf1\le\mathbf1\), the total-variation dual inequality
   445: gives
   446: \[
   447: \left|(\nu P^L-\pi)M_B\mathbf1\right|
   448: \le \|\nu P^L-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}} .
   449: \]
   450: 
   451: It remains only to bound the hidden-chain term.  The matrix
   452: \[
   453: P=\begin{pmatrix}p&1-p\\ b&1-b\end{pmatrix}
   454: \]
   455: has strictly positive off-diagonal entries, stationary law
   456: \(\pi=(b/(1-p+b),(1-p)/(1-p+b))\), and nontrivial eigenvalue
   457: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\).  By
   458: \Cref{lem:hidden-mode-contraction-main}, \(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1\).
   459: On the two-state zero-sum subspace, multiplication by \(P\) is multiplication
   460: by \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  Hence, for every initial law \(\nu\),
   461: \[
   462: \|\nu P^L-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
   463: \le C|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|^L
   464: \]
   465: with a finite constant \(C\) uniform over \(\nu\).  Taking any
   466: \(\theta\in(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|,1)\), or \(\theta=|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|\)
   467: when \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\ne0\) and increasing \(C\) if desired, proves
   468: \eqref{eq:finite-hidden-start-path-tv-main}.
   469: 
   470: The final assertion follows because every statistic of the retained word is a
   471: measurable image of that \(N\)-word.  Total variation cannot increase under a
   472: measurable map, so the law of \(S_N\) under the finite hidden start differs
   473: from its stationary-start law by at most \(C\theta^{L_N}\to0\).  Applying this
   474: bound to continuity sets gives distributional convergence; applying it to the
   475: events \(\{|S_N-s|>\varepsilon\}\), or to the events appearing in a stated
   476: probability limit, gives convergence in probability and event-probability
   477: limits.
   478: \end{proof}
   479: 
   480: \begin{lemma}[Hazard obstruction to finite symbolic memory]\label{lem:hazard-obstruction-main}
   481: A renewal binary process with full gap support is a finite-order Markov chain
   482: only if its gap hazard is eventually constant.
   483: \end{lemma}
   484: 
   485: \begin{proof}
   486: For a renewal process, after observing \(k\) consecutive zeros since the last
   487: click, the conditional click probability is the zero-run hazard \(h_k\).
   488: If an \(r\)-step Markov rule generated the same full-support law, then all
   489: histories with \(k\ge r\) trailing zeros would have the same last \(r\) symbols
   490: and hence the same click probability.  Therefore \(h_k\) would be constant for
   491: all \(k\ge r\).
   492: 
   493: This condition concerns a transition rule conditioned on the most recent
   494: symbols.  It is different from finite dependence of separated sigma-fields:
   495: \(1\)-dependence says that events separated by one unobserved sample
   496: factorize, while an \(r\)-step Markov rule says that every future one-step
   497: transition probability is a function of only the last \(r\) symbols.  For a
   498: renewal record with full gap support, the latter would force the click
   499: probability after a long zero run to stop depending on the run length.
   500: \end{proof}
   501: 
   502: \begin{lemma}[Cylinder factorization at the memory threshold]\label{lem:kernel-factorization-main}
   503: If \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), then \(P=T_0+T_1=\mathbf1\pi\).  More
   504: explicitly, let \(u=(u_0,\ldots,u_{r-1})\) and
   505: \(v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{\ell-1})\) be arbitrary finite binary words and write
   506: \(T_u=T_{u_0}\cdots T_{u_{r-1}}\), \(T_v=T_{v_0}\cdots T_{v_{\ell-1}}\).
   507: If the word \(u\) is observed on one block and \(v\) is observed on a second
   508: block beginning two samples later, with the intervening sample unobserved,
   509: then
   510: \[
   511: \PP(A_0^{r-1}=u,\ A_{r+1}^{r+\ell}=v)
   512: =\pi T_u P T_v\mathbf1
   513: =\bigl(\pi T_u\mathbf1\bigr)\bigl(\pi T_v\mathbf1\bigr).
   514: \]
   515: Consequently every two visible blocks separated by one unobserved sample are
   516: independent, so the visible record is exact \(1\)-dependent.
   517: \end{lemma}
   518: 
   519: \begin{proof}
   520: The second eigenvalue of \(P\) is \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\), while the first is
   521: \(1\).  At \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), the primitive stochastic matrix
   522: \(P\) has rank one and both rows equal \(\pi\), hence \(P=\mathbf1\pi\).
   523: For fixed words \(u\) and \(v\), summing over the unobserved sample between
   524: the two displayed blocks inserts \(T_0+T_1=P\).  The stationary cylinder
   525: probability is therefore
   526: \[
   527: \pi T_u(T_0+T_1)T_v\mathbf1=\pi T_uPT_v\mathbf1.
   528: \]
   529: At the threshold,
   530: \[
   531: \pi T_uPT_v\mathbf1
   532: =\pi T_u\mathbf1\,\pi T_v\mathbf1
   533: =\PP(A_0^{r-1}=u)\PP(A_0^{\ell-1}=v),
   534: \]
   535: where the last equality is stationarity applied to the two single-block
   536: cylinders.  Thus every pair of finite cylinder events supported on two
   537: finite blocks with one omitted sample between them factorizes.  Translating
   538: the indices gives the same statement for any blocks
   539: \(\{i,\ldots,m\}\) and \(\{m+2,\ldots,j\}\).  Since finite cylinders generate
   540: the corresponding finite-block sigma algebras, the two finite-block sigma
   541: algebras are independent.  Letting the left and right block lengths increase
   542: and using the monotone-class theorem gives independence of
   543: \(\sigma(A_j:j\le m)\) and \(\sigma(A_j:j\ge m+2)\) for every \(m\).
   544: \end{proof}
   545: 
   546: \begin{lemma}[Exact \texorpdfstring{\(1\)}{1}-dependence criterion]\label{lem:one-dep-criterion-main}
   547: The constrained D-MAP record is exact \(1\)-dependent iff
   548: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), equivalently
   549: \(\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau)\).
   550: \end{lemma}
   551: 
   552: \begin{proof}
   553: The forward direction at the threshold is
   554: \Cref{lem:kernel-factorization-main}.  Conversely, exact \(1\)-dependence
   555: would make \(A_0\) and \(A_2\) independent.  But
   556: \[
   557: \operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_2)=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}},
   558: \]
   559: and the prefactor is nonzero by \Cref{lem:stationary-main}.
   560: \end{proof}
   561: 
   562: \begin{lemma}[Survival tails and hazard monotonicity]\label{lem:survival-hazard-main}
   563: For \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\ne\Gamma\),
   564: \begin{equation}\label{eq:survival-tail-main}
   565: S_k=\PP(G\ge k)=
   566: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   567: \end{equation}
   568: and
   569: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hazard-main}
   570: h_k=\PP(G=k\mid G\ge k)=
   571: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   572:      {\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k}.
   573: \end{equation}
   574: The equal-rate limit is
   575: \begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-rate-survival-hazard-main}
   576: \PP(G\ge k)=p^k(1+\Gamma\Delta\tau\,k),
   577: \qquad
   578: h_k=
   579: \frac{(1-p)+\Gamma\Delta\tau(k(1-p)-p)}
   580:      {1+\Gamma\Delta\tau\,k}.
   581: \end{equation}
   582: In all cases \(h_{k+1}>h_k\).
   583: \end{lemma}
   584: 
   585: \begin{proof}
   586: Summing \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main} over \(j\ge k\) gives
   587: \eqref{eq:survival-tail-main}.  Since \(g_k=S_k-S_{k+1}\), the hazard has the
   588: equivalent survival-ratio form
   589: \[
   590: h_k=\frac{g_k}{S_k}=1-\frac{S_{k+1}}{S_k}.
   591: \]
   592: Substituting \eqref{eq:survival-tail-main} into this identity gives
   593: \eqref{eq:hazard-main}.  The equal-rate display is the continuous limit.
   594: For the unequal-rate case, subtracting the two survival ratios gives the
   595: standalone identity
   596: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hazard-increment-sign-main}
   597: h_{k+1}-h_k
   598: =
   599: \frac{
   600:   \Gamma\kappa_{\mathrm r}\,
   601:   (p-s)^2\,p^k s^k
   602: }{
   603:   \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k\bigr)
   604:   \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^{k+1}-\Gamma s^{k+1}\bigr)
   605: }.
   606: \end{equation}
   607: The numerator in \eqref{eq:hazard-increment-sign-main} is positive because
   608: \(\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r}>0\), \(p,s\in(0,1)\), and the explicit square
   609: \((p-s)^2\) is positive off the equal-rate diagonal.  For the denominator,
   610: \[
   611: \kappa_{\mathrm r}p^m-\Gamma s^m
   612: =(\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma)S_m,\qquad m=k,k+1,
   613: \]
   614: by \eqref{eq:survival-tail-main}.  Since \(S_m=\PP(G\ge m)>0\), both
   615: denominator factors have sign \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma\), so their product
   616: is positive.  Equivalently, their product is
   617: \((\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma)^2S_kS_{k+1}>0\).
   618: 
   619: On the equal-rate diagonal, write
   620: \(y=\Gamma\Delta\tau=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\) and
   621: \(p=e^{-y}\).  The displayed equal-rate hazard can be rewritten as
   622: \[
   623: h_k
   624: =1-p-\frac{y p}{1+y k}.
   625: \]
   626: Thus
   627: \[
   628: h_{k+1}-h_k
   629: =yp\left(\frac1{1+yk}-\frac1{1+y(k+1)}\right)
   630: =\frac{y^2p}{(1+yk)(1+y(k+1))}>0.
   631: \]
   632: \end{proof}
   633: 
   634: \begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{prop:main-record-wrapper-detailed}]
   635: The kernels are those of \Cref{prop:instrument-kernels-main}.  Both nonzero
   636: entries of \(T_1\) land in the \(D\)-column.  Thus, after a click, the next
   637: hidden state is \(D\), independently of the pre-click hidden state and of the
   638: earlier visible record.  Click epochs are therefore regeneration epochs for
   639: the future visible record, and a Palm gap with \(k\) intervening zeros has
   640: probability
   641: \[
   642: g_k=\PP(G=k)=e_D^\top T_0^kT_1\mathbf1,\qquad k\ge0 .
   643: \]
   644: Here
   645: \[
   646: T_0=\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\ b&s\end{pmatrix},
   647: \qquad
   648: T_1\mathbf1=(1-p,a)^\top .
   649: \]
   650: For \(k\ge1\), lower-triangular multiplication gives
   651: \[
   652: T_0^k=
   653: \begin{pmatrix}
   654: p^k&0\\
   655: b\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}&s^k
   656: \end{pmatrix};
   657: \]
   658: for \(k=0\), the same formula is read with the sum empty and \(T_0^0=I\).
   659: Consequently
   660: \[
   661: g_k=b(1-p)\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}+as^k ,
   662: \]
   663: including \(g_0=a\).  If \(p\ne s\), then
   664: \[
   665: \sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}=\frac{p^k-s^k}{p-s}.
   666: \]
   667: Using the scalar identities
   668: \[
   669: b=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(p-s)}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   670: \qquad
   671: a=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)-\Gamma(1-s)}
   672:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   673: \]
   674: one obtains
   675: \[
   676: \begin{aligned}
   677: g_k
   678: &=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)(p^k-s^k)}
   679:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
   680:   +\frac{\{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)-\Gamma(1-s)\}s^k}
   681:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma} \\
   682: &=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   683:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   684: \end{aligned}
   685: \]
   686: This proves the displayed off-diagonal gap law.  The case \(p=s\), equivalently
   687: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), is not obtained by dividing by \(p-s\); it is
   688: the continuous equal-rate limit recorded in
   689: \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}.  Since the path that remains in \(D\)
   690: for \(k\) zero-labelled steps and then clicks has probability \(s^ka>0\), every
   691: \(g_k\) is positive.  Thus the renewal gap law has full support.
   692: 
   693: For the hazard, summing the just-proved gap law gives, off the equal-rate
   694: diagonal,
   695: \[
   696: S_k=\PP(G\ge k)=
   697: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   698: \]
   699: Since \(g_k=S_k-S_{k+1}\),
   700: \[
   701: h_k=\frac{g_k}{S_k}=1-\frac{S_{k+1}}{S_k}.
   702: \]
   703: This hazard formula is a population diagnostic.  Substitution yields the
   704: theorem's hazard formula and the increment identity
   705: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hazard-increment-theorem-main}
   706: h_{k+1}-h_k
   707: =
   708: \frac{
   709:   \Gamma\kappa_{\mathrm r}\,
   710:   (p-s)^2\,p^k s^k
   711: }{
   712:   \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k\bigr)
   713:   \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^{k+1}-\Gamma s^{k+1}\bigr)
   714: }.
   715: \end{equation}
   716: The numerator in \eqref{eq:hazard-increment-theorem-main} is positive because
   717: \(\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r}>0\), \(p,s\in(0,1)\), and \((p-s)^2>0\) off the
   718: diagonal.  The denominator factors satisfy
   719: \[
   720: \kappa_{\mathrm r}p^m-\Gamma s^m
   721: =(\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma)S_m,\qquad m=k,k+1.
   722: \]
   723: Both denominator factors have sign \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma\), so their
   724: product is positive.  Equivalently,
   725: \[
   726: \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k\bigr)
   727: \bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^{k+1}-\Gamma s^{k+1}\bigr)
   728: =(\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma)^2S_kS_{k+1}>0.
   729: \]
   730: Thus \(h_{k+1}>h_k\) off the diagonal.  On the equal-rate diagonal,
   731: \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main} gives
   732: \[
   733: h_k=1-p-\frac{yp}{1+yk},\qquad
   734: h_{k+1}-h_k=
   735: \frac{y^2p}{(1+yk)(1+y(k+1))}>0,
   736: \]
   737: where \(y=\Gamma\Delta\tau=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\).  Hence the
   738: zero-run hazard is strictly increasing in all positive parameter cases.
   739: 
   740: It remains to verify the covariance and dependence assertions.  Let
   741: \(P=T_0+T_1\) and \(u=T_1\mathbf1=(1-p,a)^\top\).  By
   742: \Cref{lem:stationary-main}, the stationary hidden law is \(\pi\), the click
   743: density is \(\rho=\pi u\), and \(a-\rho\ne0\).  The two eigenvalues of \(P\)
   744: are \(1\) and \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\).  The centered click vector lies
   745: in the hidden eigenspace:
   746: \[
   747: u-\rho\mathbf1=\frac{1-p-a}{1-p+b}\binom{1-p}{-b},
   748: \qquad
   749: P^{n-1}u=\rho\mathbf1+
   750: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}(u-\rho\mathbf1).
   751: \]
   752: Conditioning on \(A_0=1\) again leaves the next hidden state equal to \(D\);
   753: therefore
   754: \[
   755: \PP(A_n=1\mid A_0=1)
   756: =e_D^\top P^{n-1}u
   757: =\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}.
   758: \]
   759: Multiplying by \(\PP(A_0=1)=\rho\) and subtracting \(\rho^2\) gives
   760: \[
   761: \gamma_n=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1},\qquad n\ge1 .
   762: \]
   763: 
   764: If \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), then \(\det P=0\).  Since \(P\) is stochastic,
   765: both rows of \(P\) equal the stationary row \(\pi\), so \(P=\mathbf1\pi\).
   766: Summing over one unobserved sample between two visible blocks inserts this
   767: rank-one matrix and factorizes the two cylinder probabilities, as in
   768: \Cref{lem:kernel-factorization-main}; hence the visible record is
   769: \(1\)-dependent.  Conversely, \(1\)-dependence makes \(A_0\) and \(A_2\)
   770: independent, so \(0=\gamma_2=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  The
   771: prefactor is nonzero by \Cref{lem:stationary-main}, and hence
   772: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\).
   773: 
   774: Finally, suppose the renewal record were an \(r\)-step Markov chain.  Because
   775: all finite zero runs have positive probability, the conditional click
   776: probability after a run of \(k\) zeros would depend only on the final \(r\)
   777: zeros once \(k\ge r\).  Thus \(h_k\) would be eventually constant.  The strict
   778: increase proved above rules this out for every \(r\), so the visible process
   779: is not a finite-order Markov chain.
   780: \end{proof}
   781: 
   782: \begin{proposition}[Threshold finite-window count variance]\label{prop:threshold-finite-window-count-main}
   783: Fix the constrained D-MAP kernels of
   784: \Cref{prop:instrument-kernels-main} in stationarity and suppose that the
   785: hidden transfer mode is at the exact-dependence threshold
   786: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\).  For
   787: \[
   788: N_m=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}A_n,\qquad m\ge1,
   789: \]
   790: one has the exact finite-window identity
   791: \begin{equation}\label{eq:threshold-finite-window-count-main}
   792: \Var(N_m)
   793: =m\rho(1-\rho)+2(m-1)\rho(a-\rho).
   794: \end{equation}
   795: Equivalently, the finite-window variance is the one-dependent covariance
   796: sum with only the adjacent covariance retained.
   797: 
   798: The paper uses only \eqref{eq:threshold-finite-window-count-main}.  Higher
   799: finite-window count laws at the threshold are obtained from the ordinary
   800: probability generating polynomial
   801: \[
   802: P_m(z)=\EE z^{N_m}
   803: =\sum_{u\in\{0,1\}^m}z^{u_0+\cdots+u_{m-1}}
   804:   \,\PP(A_0=u_0,\ldots,A_{m-1}=u_{m-1}),
   805: \]
   806: with the cylinder probabilities evaluated by the renewal inclusion product
   807: of \Cref{cor:renewal-principal-minor-word-law-main}.  This is the finite-window
   808: specialization of the standard \(1\)-dependent count generating-function
   809: bookkeeping; no separate closed formula for every higher count law is used in
   810: the sequel.
   811: \end{proposition}
   812: 
   813: \begin{proof}
   814: Stationarity gives the exact variance decomposition
   815: \[
   816: \Var(N_m)
   817: =m\gamma_0+2\sum_{h=1}^{m-1}(m-h)\gamma_h,
   818: \qquad
   819: \gamma_h=\operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_h).
   820: \]
   821: Here \(\gamma_0=\rho(1-\rho)\).  The covariance tail proved in
   822: \Cref{thm:main-record-wrapper} is
   823: \[
   824: \gamma_h=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,h-1},
   825: \qquad h\ge1.
   826: \]
   827: At \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), exact \(1\)-dependence is
   828: \Cref{lem:one-dep-criterion-main} and the displayed tail leaves
   829: \(\gamma_1=\rho(a-\rho)\) and \(\gamma_h=0\) for every \(h\ge2\).
   830: The strict inequality \(\gamma_1<0\), proved in
   831: \Cref{lem:stationary-main}, excludes \(0\)-dependence.
   832: Substitution
   833: in the stationary variance sum gives
   834: \[
   835: \Var(N_m)
   836: =m\rho(1-\rho)+2(m-1)\rho(a-\rho),
   837: \]
   838: which is \eqref{eq:threshold-finite-window-count-main}.
   839: 
   840: For the final assertion, the polynomial \(P_m\) is just the definition of the
   841: finite-window count distribution.  At the threshold the law is \(1\)-dependent
   842: by \Cref{lem:one-dep-criterion-main}; the same finite cylinder probabilities
   843: are explicitly determined from renewal inclusion products in
   844: \Cref{cor:renewal-principal-minor-word-law-main}.  Taking coefficients of \(P_m\) therefore
   845: recovers the count probabilities.
   846: \end{proof}
   847: 
   848: \begin{lemma}[Discrete renewal count-variance identity]\label{lem:discrete-renewal-fano-main}
   849: Let \((A_n)_{n\in\mathbb Z}\) be a stationary binary renewal process on the
   850: sample lattice, with click epochs separated by i.i.d. integer cycle lengths

 succeeded in 499ms:
  3700: \(1-\delta_N\).  On that auxiliary event, the same interval comparison of
  3701: \([Q]\) with the supplied critical value proves the stated transcript-valid
  3702: accept, reject, or undecided implication.  The domination probability is conditional on the
  3703: auxiliary Gaussian simulation, so its record-level interpretation is exactly
  3704: the product-space statement proved in
  3705: \Cref{lem:conditional-monte-carlo-quantile-main}, combined separately with
  3706: \Cref{thm:e-fixed-candidate-exchange-diagonal-main}; it is not a finite-sample
  3707: coverage claim for the retained record alone.
  3708: \end{proof}
  3709: \else
  3710: \begin{remark}[Supplementary finite-comparison certificate interface]\label{thm:certified-diagonal-inference-main}\label{prop:projected-gaussian-certificate-interface-main}
  3711: The finite tuple that would be needed for a realized diagonal
  3712: accept/reject/undecided comparison is supplementary implementation material,
  3713: not part of the delivered main theorem chain.  The main article uses only the
  3714: population branch certificate at \(z_0=1/2\) and the ideal fixed-null
  3715: asymptotics of Theorem~E.  It prints no record-specific critical-value bracket
  3716: \([L,U]\), no statistic enclosure \([Q]\), and no complete finite comparison
  3717: tuple.
  3718: \end{remark}
  3719: \fi
  3720: 
  3721: \fi
  3722: \begin{lemma}[Projected-Gaussian continuity and rank degeneracy]\label{thm:projected-gaussian-continuity-degeneracy-main}
  3723: Fix \(0<z<1\), put
  3724: \[
  3725: n_z=(-z,1)^\top,\qquad H_z=\{h:n_z^\top h\le0\},
  3726: \]
  3727: and let
  3728: \[
  3729: T_z=(\Pi_{H_z}Z)^\top\Sigma^+\Pi_{H_z}Z,
  3730: \qquad Z\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma),
  3731: \]
  3732: where \(\Sigma\) is a nonnegative definite \(2\times2\) matrix.  Then the
  3733: distribution of \(T_z\) is continuous in the following cases:
  3734: \[
  3735: \operatorname{rank}\Sigma=2,
  3736: \qquad\text{or}\qquad
  3737: \operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1
  3738: \text{ and }
  3739: \operatorname{Range}\Sigma\ne \operatorname{span}\{n_z\}.
  3740: \]
  3741: If \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1\) and
  3742: \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma=\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), then
  3743: \[
  3744: \PP(T_z=0)=\frac12
  3745: \]
  3746: and \(T_z\) has no atom on \((0,\infty)\).  More precisely,
  3747: \[
  3748: T_z\sim \frac12\delta_0+\frac12\chi^2_1 .
  3749: \]
  3750: Equivalently,
  3751: \[
  3752: \PP(T_z=0)=\frac12,\qquad
  3753: \PP(T_z\le t)=\frac12+\frac12F_{\chi^2_1}(t),\quad t>0.
  3754: \]
  3755: Consequently, for a lower quantile level \(0<p_c<1\), the lower
  3756: \(p_c\)-quantile is
  3757: \[
  3758: c_{p_c}=0\quad\text{if }p_c\le\frac12,\qquad
  3759: c_{p_c}=F_{\chi^2_1}^{-1}(2p_c-1)\quad\text{if }p_c>\frac12,
  3760: \]
  3761: and the positive-quantile case is a continuity point of the law of \(T_z\).  If
  3762: \(\Sigma=0\), then
  3763: \(T_z=0\) almost surely.  Consequently the continuity condition in
  3764: \Cref{lem:projected-gaussian-bootstrap-halfspace-main} is automatic except
  3765: for the zero covariance case and for the rank-one case in which the retained
  3766: Gaussian direction is normal to the exchange-diagonal tangent half-space; in
  3767: that rank-one normal case the only discontinuity is the atom at zero.
  3768: \end{lemma}
  3769: 
  3770: \begin{proof}
  3771: If \(\Sigma=0\), the assertion is immediate.  Suppose first that
  3772: \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=2\).  The Gaussian law has a Lebesgue density on
  3773: \(\RR^2\).  On \(H_z\), the statistic is the nonzero quadratic form
  3774: \(u^\top\Sigma^+u\).  On \(H_z^c\), the projection is the linear map
  3775: \[
  3776: L_z u=u-\frac{n_z^\top u}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z
  3777: \]
  3778: onto the boundary line \(n_z^\top h=0\), and the statistic is the quadratic
  3779: form \(u^\top L_z^\top\Sigma^+L_z u\), not identically zero on that half-plane.
  3780: For each fixed \(c\), the level set of either nonzero quadratic polynomial has
  3781: Lebesgue measure zero.  Hence \(\PP(T_z=c)=0\) for every \(c\).
  3782: 
  3783: Now let \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1\).  Write
  3784: \(\Sigma=\lambda vv^\top\), where \(\lambda>0\) and \(\|v\|=1\).  Then
  3785: \(Z=\xi v\), with \(\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,\lambda)\), and
  3786: \[
  3787: \Sigma^+=\lambda^{-1}vv^\top .
  3788: \]
  3789: If \(n_z^\top v=0\), the line
  3790: \(\operatorname{span}\{v\}\) lies in the boundary of \(H_z\), so
  3791: \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\), which has a continuous \(\chi^2_1\) law.  If
  3792: \(n_z^\top v\ne0\), one half-line is already in \(H_z\), where again
  3793: \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\).  On the other half-line,
  3794: \[
  3795: \Pi_{H_z}(\xi v)=
  3796: \xi\left(v-\frac{n_z^\top v}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z\right).
  3797: \]
  3798: The corresponding coefficient in the seminorm \(\Sigma^+\) is
  3799: \[
  3800: \lambda^{-1}
  3801: \left\{v^\top\left(v-\frac{n_z^\top v}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z\right)\right\}^2 .
  3802: \]
  3803: This coefficient is positive unless \(v\) is parallel to \(n_z\).  Therefore,
  3804: when \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma\ne\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), \(T_z\) is
  3805: a two-sided mixture of positive multiples of \(\xi^2\) and has no atoms.  When
  3806:   \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma=\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), the outside
  3807:   half-line projects to the origin and contributes mass \(1/2\) at zero, while
  3808:   the inside half-line gives \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\).  Conditional on this
  3809:   half-line, \(\xi^2/\lambda\) has the \(\chi^2_1\) law.  Hence the distribution
  3810:   is \(\frac12\delta_0+\frac12\chi^2_1\), and the stated lower-quantile split
  3811:   follows immediately from its distribution function
  3812:   \(F(t)=1/2+\PP\{\chi^2_1\le t\}/2\) for \(t>0\).  This proves the rank-one
  3813:   alternatives.
  3814: \end{proof}
  3815: 
  3816: \begin{proposition}[Computable diagonal covariance]\label{prop:computable-diagonal-covariance-main}
  3817: Work at a fixed constrained D-MAP exchange-diagonal population point
  3818: \(\sigma_z=(2z,z^2)\), \(0<z<1\), equivalently \(p=s=z\).  Put
  3819: \[
  3820: \theta=-\log z.
  3821: \]
  3822: Then the diagonal Palm gap law is
  3823: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-gap-mass-computable-main}
  3824: g_k=\PP(G=k)
  3825: =z^k\{(1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\},\qquad k\ge0 .
  3826: \end{equation}
  3827: In particular
  3828: \[
  3829: g_0=(1-z)-\theta z,\qquad
  3830: g_1=z\{(1-z)(1+\theta)-\theta z\}.
  3831: \]
  3832: Define
  3833: \[
  3834: \mu=\frac1{1-z}+\frac{\theta z}{(1-z)^2},
  3835: \qquad
  3836: \nu=\frac{1+z}{(1-z)^2}
  3837:      +\frac{\theta z(3+z)}{(1-z)^3},
  3838: \qquad
  3839: m_2=g_1+g_0^2 .
  3840: \]
  3841: Then the three inclusion coordinates at this diagonal point are
  3842: \[
  3843: \Rinc(z)=\frac1\mu(1,g_0,m_2)^\top ,
  3844: \]
  3845: and the quotient denominator is
  3846: \[
  3847: \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}(z)=r_1(z)-r_0(z)^2
  3848: =-\frac{\theta^2z^2(1-z)^2}{(1-z+\theta z)^2}<0 .
  3849: \]
  3850: For the cycle reward variables
  3851: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-cycle-reward-y-main}
  3852: U_j=\left(1,\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0\}},
  3853: \mathbf1_{\{G_j=1\}}+\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0,G_{j+1}=0\}}\right)^\top,
  3854: \qquad
  3855: L_j=G_j+1,\qquad
  3856: Y_j=U_j-\Rinc(z)L_j ,
  3857: \end{equation}
  3858: the \(Y_j\) sequence is one-dependent, centered, and has covariance
  3859: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-sigma-r-y-main}
  3860: \Sigma_r(z)=\frac1{\mu(z)}
  3861: \left[\operatorname{Var}(Y_0)+\operatorname{Cov}(Y_0,Y_1)
  3862: +\operatorname{Cov}(Y_1,Y_0)\right].
  3863: \end{equation}
  3864: Every entry in \eqref{eq:diagonal-sigma-r-y-main} is an absolutely convergent
  3865: finite or double sum under the explicit masses \eqref{eq:diagonal-gap-mass-computable-main};
  3866: indeed the indicators in \(U_j\) restrict the nontrivial terms to
  3867: \(G_j\in\{0,1\}\), while the factors containing \(L_j\) are sums of
  3868: polynomials in \(k\) against \(g_k=O(kz^k)\).
  3869: Let
  3870: \[
  3871: A_z=
  3872: \begin{pmatrix}
  3873: 1&g_0&m_2\\
  3874: g_0&g_0&g_0^2\\
  3875: m_2&g_0^2&m_2
  3876: \end{pmatrix},
  3877: \quad
  3878: e_z=
  3879: \begin{pmatrix}
  3880: \mu\\ g_0\\ 2g_1+g_0^2
  3881: \end{pmatrix},
  3882: \quad
  3883: d_z=
  3884: \begin{pmatrix}
  3885: \mu\\ \mu g_0\\ \mu g_1+g_0^2
  3886: \end{pmatrix},
  3887: \]
  3888: and
  3889: \[
  3890: B_z=
  3891: \begin{pmatrix}
  3892: 1&g_0&m_2\\
  3893: g_0&g_0^2&g_0m_2\\
  3894: m_2&g_0(g_1+g_0)&g_1^2+g_1g_0^2+g_0^3
  3895: \end{pmatrix}.
  3896: \]
  3897: With \(R=\Rinc(z)\), the complete-cycle inclusion covariance on the exchange
  3898: diagonal is the finite expression
  3899: \begin{equation}\label{eq:dmap-diagonal-sigma-r-finite-main}
  3900: \Sigma_r(z)=\frac1\mu\left\{
  3901: A_z-e_zR^\top-Re_z^\top+\nu RR^\top
  3902: +B_z-d_zR^\top+B_z^\top-Rd_z^\top
  3903: \right\}.
  3904: \end{equation}
  3905: Consequently the actual diagonal covariance used by the projected-Gaussian
  3906: statistic is the reproducible \(2\times2\) matrix
  3907: \begin{equation}\label{eq:dmap-diagonal-sigma-finite-main}
  3908: \Sigma_\sigma(z)=D\Phi(\Rinc(z))\,\Sigma_r(z)\,D\Phi(\Rinc(z))^\top
  3909: \end{equation}
  3910: where \(D\Phi\) is the explicit Jacobian in
  3911: \eqref{eq:lambda-gradient-main}--\eqref{eq:phi-jacobian-main}, evaluated at
  3912: the displayed \(\Rinc(z)\).  No infinite covariance-tail truncation or simulation
  3913: input is hidden in \eqref{eq:dmap-diagonal-sigma-finite-main}: for a fixed
  3914: \(z\), all entries are obtained by ordinary arithmetic from
  3915: \((z,\theta,g_0,g_1)\), equivalently from the absolutely convergent
  3916: finite/double sums in \eqref{eq:diagonal-sigma-r-y-main}.
  3917: \end{proposition}
  3918: 
  3919: \begin{proof}
  3920: On the equal-rate diagonal the gap law is the one in
  3921: \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}, which gives
  3922: \eqref{eq:diagonal-gap-mass-computable-main}.  Hence
  3923: \[
  3924: \EE(G+1)=\sum_{k\ge0}\PP(G\ge k)=\mu,\qquad
  3925: \EE(G+1)^2=\sum_{k\ge0}(2k+1)\PP(G\ge k)=\nu ,
  3926: \]
  3927: which gives the displayed closed forms by summing the two elementary geometric
  3928: series and their first two derivatives.
  3929: 
  3930: For the inclusion-cycle reward in
  3931: \Cref{lem:three-inclusion-clt-reduction-main},
  3932: \[
  3933: U_j=\left(1,\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0\}},
  3934: \mathbf1_{\{G_j=1\}}+\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0,G_{j+1}=0\}}\right)^\top .
  3935: \]
  3936: Since the gaps are i.i.d., \(\EE U_j=(1,g_0,g_1+g_0^2)^\top\), proving
  3937: \(\Rinc(z)=\EE U_j/\mu\).  The quotient denominator formula follows by direct
  3938: substitution and simplification; it is strictly negative because
  3939: \(0<z<1\) and \(\theta>0\).
  3940: 
  3941: The matrix \(A_z\) is \(\EE(U_0U_0^\top)\), \(e_z=\EE((G_0+1)U_0)\), and
  3942: \(B_z=\EE(U_0U_1^\top)\).  The vector \(d_z=\EE(U_0(G_1+1))\); the only
  3943: non-factorized entry is the third component, where
  3944: \[
  3945: \EE\{(\mathbf1_{\{G_0=1\}}+\mathbf1_{\{G_0=0,G_1=0\}})(G_1+1)\}
  3946: =\mu g_1+g_0^2 .
  3947: \]
  3948: Writing \(Y_j=U_j-\Rinc(z)(G_j+1)\), the reward sequence is one-dependent and
  3949: \[
  3950: \Sigma_r(z)
  3951: =\frac1\mu\{\EE(Y_0Y_0^\top)+\EE(Y_0Y_1^\top)+\EE(Y_1Y_0^\top)\}.
  3952: \]
  3953: Expanding the three expectations gives exactly
  3954: \eqref{eq:dmap-diagonal-sigma-r-finite-main}.  The last display is then the
  3955: delta-method sandwich for the symmetric quotient coordinates
  3956: \(\Phi(\Rinc(z))=(p+s,ps)\), whose Jacobian was computed in
  3957: \eqref{eq:lambda-gradient-main}--\eqref{eq:phi-jacobian-main}.
  3958: \end{proof}
  3959: 
  3960: The main theorem uses
  3961: \Cref{lem:dmap-diagonal-covariance-alternatives-main} only through the printed
  3962: branch certificate in
  3963: \Cref{app:self-contained-diagonal-branch-certificate-main}.  The complete
  3964: interval transcript and the outward-rounded verifier are deliberately outside
  3965: the mathematical flow of this section; see
  3966: \Cref{def:dmap-diagonal-rank-certificate-main,lem:diagonal-interval-branch-certificate-main,rem:worked-diagonal-branch-certificate-main}.
  3967:

  3971: verifies the renewal hypothesis for this model.  Theorem~C then states the
  3972: exact finite-record statistic for the three inclusion coordinates and applies
  3973: the generic complete-cycle CLT to that statistic.

  1230: \]

  1234: \begin{lemma}[Exchange symmetry in dimensionless survival coordinates]\label{lem:gap-law-exchange-symmetry-main}
  1235: Put
  1236: \[
  1237: x=-\log p=\Gamma\Delta\tau,\qquad
  1238: y=-\log s=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau,
  1239: \qquad p=e^{-x},\qquad s=e^{-y}.
  1240: \]
  1241: For \(x\ne y\), the zero-run law in \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main} is
  1242: \begin{equation}\label{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}
  1243: g_k=
  1244: \frac{y(1-p)p^k-x(1-s)s^k}{y-x},
  1245: \qquad k\ge0.
  1246: \end{equation}
  1247: The hidden eigenvalue in \eqref{eq:lambda-hid-main} is, in the same
  1248: coordinates,
  1249: \begin{equation}\label{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main}
  1250: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}
  1251: =\frac{y s-x p}{y-x}
  1252: =\frac{y e^{-y}-x e^{-x}}{y-x}.
  1253: \end{equation}
  1254: Both \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main} and
  1255: \eqref{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main} are invariant under the simultaneous
  1256: exchange \((x,p)\leftrightarrow(y,s)\).  The equal-rate laws are their
  1257: continuous limits.  Consequently the stationary visible renewal law is
  1258: unchanged by exchanging \(\Gamma\) and \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\).
  1259: \end{lemma}
  1260: 
  1261: \begin{proof}
  1262: Multiplying numerator and denominator in \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main} by
  1263: \(\Delta\tau\) gives \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}.  Similarly,
  1264: \eqref{eq:lambda-hid-main} becomes
  1265: \[
  1266: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}
  1267: =\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}s-\Gamma p}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
  1268: =\frac{y s-x p}{y-x}.
  1269: \]
  1270: Under \((x,p)\leftrightarrow(y,s)\), the numerator
  1271: \[
  1272: y(1-p)p^k-x(1-s)s^k
  1273: \]
  1274: and the denominator \(y-x\) both change sign, so \(g_k\) is unchanged.  The
  1275: same sign cancellation gives
  1276: \[
  1277: y s-x p\longmapsto x p-y s=-(y s-x p),
  1278: \qquad
  1279: y-x\longmapsto x-y=-(y-x),
  1280: \]
  1281: and hence \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\) is unchanged.  The diagonal formulas
  1282: follow by taking the continuous limits \(x\to y\).  Since
  1283: \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main} proves that the visible law is the stationary
  1284: renewal law with gap probabilities \(g_k\), the unordered pair
  1285: \(\{(x,p),(y,s)\}\) determines the whole gap distribution.  It also determines
  1286: the stationary renewal intensity \(1/\EE(G+1)\).  Therefore the stationary
  1287: renewal measures generated before and after the exchange have the same
  1288: finite-dimensional visible distributions.
  1289: \end{proof}
  1290: 
  1291: \begin{convention}\label{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}
  1292: \textbf{Identifiable estimands and branch-map convention.}
  1293: From one stationary visible record the identifiable objects are the inclusion
  1294: vector \((r_0,r_1,r_2)\), the quotient
  1295: \(\Phi(r)=(p+s,ps)\) on
  1296: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}=r_1-r_0^2\ne0\), the unordered survival-factor pair
  1297: \(\{p,s\}\), and visible functionals such as
  1298: \(\gamma_2=\operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_2)\).  These are quotient-level objects:
  1299: the visible inverse does not order the two roots and does not by itself
  1300: produce labelled attempt/recovery parameters.  When the sampling interval is
  1301: a known design interval, the unordered numerical rate set
  1302: \[
  1303: \left\{-\frac{\log p}{\Delta\tau},
  1304:        -\frac{\log s}{\Delta\tau}\right\}
  1305: \]
  1306: is visible-law identifiable, but the ordered vector
  1307: \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\) is not.  If \(\Delta\tau\) is not known as a
  1308: design interval, the same quotient identifies only
  1309: \[
  1310: \{-\log p,-\log s\}
  1311: =\{\Gamma\Delta\tau,\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\},
  1312: \]
  1313: not numerical rates per unit time.  A separated ordered labelled-rate vector
  1314: may be reported only after an additional side-data branch map
  1315: \(\mathcal B\), not contained in the visible record, assigns the two roots to
  1316: the attempt and recovery labels on a separated chart
  1317: \(\Delta_\sigma=(p-s)^2>0\).  Any theorem using such a vector is a
  1318: conditional side-data theorem: it first applies the quotient inverse
  1319: \(\Phi\), then applies the externally supplied branch map.  Theorem statements
  1320: condition on a deterministic branch map; estimated branch maps are reduced to
  1321: this case in \Cref{app:branch-map-oracle-reduction-main}.  On the exchange
  1322: diagonal \((2z,z^2)\), the invariant visible-law estimand is the common
  1323: dimensionless product \(-\log z\), and only for known \(\Delta\tau\) the common
  1324: numerical rate \(-\log z/\Delta\tau\), not a separated ordered chart.
  1325: \end{convention}
  1326: 
  1327: \begin{proposition}[Detailed form of Theorem~B: quotient inverse]\label{prop:toeplitz-image-inverse-duality-detailed}
  1328: Work under the constrained D-MAP record model of
  1329: \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}.  Thus \(p,s,a,b,T_0,T_1\) and the
  1330: stationary visible process \((A_n)_{n\in\mathbb Z}\) are the quantities fixed
