  1380: \]
  1381: This theorem stops at the exchange quotient; ordered
  1382: \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\) requires
  1383: \Cref{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}.  The first population singularity
  1384: is the quotient denominator
  1385: \[
  1386: \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}=r_1-r_0^2=r_0(a-r_0),
  1387: \]
  1388: which must be nonzero to recover \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  The second is
  1389: not a singularity of the quotient inverse itself: at
  1390: \(\Delta_\sigma=0\) the unordered pair simply collapses to the common root.
  1391: Only the separated-root labelled chart of
  1392: \Cref{prop:population-separated-root-labelled-chart-main} is singular there.  The
  1393: dimensionless formulas
  1394: \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}--\eqref{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main} show
  1395: that the stationary visible law generated by \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\)
  1396: equals that generated by \((\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Gamma)\).  Thus the visible
  1397: law identifies only the exchange quotient, not an ordered branch labelling.
  1398: At the exchange diagonal
  1399: \(\sigma=(2z,z^2)\), \(0<z<1\), the unordered pair collapses to the single
  1400: survival factor \(z\), so the common dimensionless product \(-\log z\), and
  1401: for known \(\Delta\tau\) the common numerical rate \(-\log z/\Delta\tau\),
  1402: remains identifiable even though no separated ordered branch is.
  1403: \end{proposition}
  1404: 
  1405: \begin{proof}
  1406: First identify the only cancellation in the inverse.  From
  1407: \[
  1408: r_0=\rho,\qquad r_1=\PP(A_0=A_1=1)=\rho a,\qquad
  1409: r_2=\PP(A_0=A_2=1)=\rho u_2
  1410: \]
  1411: and \(r_0>0\), one obtains \(a=r_1/r_0\) and \(u_2=r_2/r_0\).  The Palm
  1412: covariance identity \Cref{lem:covariance-transfer-main} gives
  1413: \[
  1414: u_2=\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}},
  1415: \]
  1416: so, since \(r_0=\rho\),
  1417: \[
  1418: r_2-r_0^2
  1419: =\rho\{\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\}-\rho^2
  1420: =\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}},
  1421: \]
  1422: while
  1423: \[
  1424: r_1-r_0^2=\rho a-\rho^2=\rho(a-\rho).
  1425: \]
  1426: For the constrained D-MAP, \(\rho>0\) and \(a-\rho<0\) by
  1427: \Cref{lem:stationary-main}.  Hence
  1428: \[
  1429: r_1-r_0^2=\rho(a-\rho)\neq0,
  1430: \]
  1431: and the quotient division is by the nonzero scalar
  1432: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}\), not by a quantity that may vanish.  Therefore
  1433: \[
  1434: \lambda
  1435: =\frac{u_2-r_0}{a-r_0}
  1436: =\frac{r_2/r_0-r_0}{r_1/r_0-r_0}
  1437: =\frac{r_2-r_0^2}{r_1-r_0^2}.
  1438: \]
  1439: The final equality uses only scalar division by \(r_0>0\) and
  1440: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}\ne0\), precisely the stated inverse domain.
  1441: 
  1442: The two coordinates of the boxed map are now computed directly.  Since
  1443: \(a=1-s-b\) and \(\lambda=\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\),
  1444: \[
  1445: \Phi_1=1-(1-s-b)+(p-b)=p+s.
  1446: \]
  1447: For the second coordinate, write \(q=1-p\).  Also
  1448: \[
  1449: 1-\lambda=1-p+b=q+b,
  1450: \]
  1451: and the stationary click probability from \Cref{lem:stationary-main} is
  1452: \[
  1453: \rho=\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}.
  1454: \]
  1455: Therefore
  1456: \[
  1457: \rho(1-\lambda)
  1458: =\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}(q+b)
  1459: =q(1-s).
  1460: \]
  1461: Since \(r_0=\rho\), substituting this value, \(a=1-s-b\), and
  1462: \(\lambda=p-b\) into the boxed formula gives the full scalar identity
  1463: \[
  1464: \begin{aligned}
  1465: \Phi_2
  1466: &=\rho(1-\lambda)-a+\lambda\\
  1467: &=q(1-s)-(1-s-b)+(p-b)\\
  1468: &=q(1-s)+p+s-1\\
  1469: &=q-qs+p+s-1\\
  1470: &=s-qs\\
  1471: &=ps .
  1472: \end{aligned}
  1473: \]
  1474: These are exactly the two identities summarized in
  1475: \Cref{lem:quotient-inverse-symmetric-algebra-main}.  Hence
  1476: \[
  1477: \{p,s\}=\text{roots of }z^2-\Phi_1z+\Phi_2=0.
  1478: \]
  1479: The unordered roots are therefore identified, but their labels are not.  If
  1480: \(\Delta\tau\) is fixed by design, applying
  1481: \(z\mapsto-\log z/\Delta\tau\) to these roots gives the unordered numerical
  1482: rate set; without that known interval the root set gives only the
  1483: dimensionless products \(\{-\log p,-\log s\}\).  Finally,
  1484: \Cref{lem:gap-law-exchange-symmetry-main} proves that swapping
  1485: the two roots also leaves the stationary renewal gap law, and hence all
  1486: finite-dimensional visible distributions, unchanged.  The inverse therefore
  1487: lands on the quotient by the exchange \((p,\Gamma)\leftrightarrow
  1488: (s,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\), not on separated labelled rates.  At a double root
  1489: the quotient itself contains the common survival factor and dimensionless
  1490: product, with the common numerical rate obtained only for known
  1491: \(\Delta\tau\), as made explicit in
  1492: \Cref{lem:diagonal-common-rate-main}.
  1493: \end{proof}
  1494: 
  1495: \begin{definition}[Projected plug-in word-law reconstruction]\label{def:projected-plugin-word-law-reconstruction-main}
  1496: A finite-record projected plug-in word-law reconstruction is a consistency
  1497: construction formed from a noisy inclusion triple only on the retained event
  1498: \(\mathcal E_N^{\rm law}\) fixed in advance.  That event is a deterministic
  1499: domain-of-definition and withholding rule inside
  1500: \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}, not a model-validation test and not
  1501: an uncertainty statement.  On that retained branch, the raw quotient coordinate
  1502: \(\Phi(\widehat r_N^{\mathrm{cyc}})\) is first projected to
  1503: \[
  1504: F=\{(x+y,xy):0\le x,y\le1\}
  1505: \]
  1506: by the tie-broken nearest-point projection
  1507: \(\widetilde\sigma_N=\Pi_F^{\rm tb}\{\Phi(\widehat r_N^{\mathrm{cyc}})\}\).
  1508: The reconstruction is the lifted renewal-consistent coordinate
  1509: \[
  1510: (\rho_*(\widetilde\sigma_N),\widetilde\sigma_N)\in
  1511: \mathcal M_{\rm ren}
  1512: :=\{(\rho_*(\sigma),\sigma):\sigma\in F\},
  1513: \]
  1514: where \(\rho_*(\widetilde\sigma_N)\) is computed from the projected root gap
  1515: law, not from the raw empirical intensity
  1516: \(\widehat r_{0,N}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\).  The associated finite-window word
  1517: probabilities are then the renewal inclusion products and finite
  1518: inclusion--exclusion probabilities of this lifted projected law.
  1519: If any denominator, projection-root, interior-root, or declared numerical
  1520: certificate required by the operational protocol fails, no projected plug-in
  1521: word probabilities are formed.  The output is a projected plug-in word-law
  1522: reconstruction only: it gives projected word probabilities as a
  1523: renewal-consistent plug-in law, not confidence intervals, bands, \(p\)-values,
  1524: or coverage statements for those word probabilities.
  1525: Algorithm~4 in \Cref{app:finite-record-implementation-protocols-main} gives the finite-window
  1526: coefficient recursion, truncation convention, scalar-mean tail certificate,
  1527: and withholding rule used for numerical reconstruction.
  1528: \end{definition}
  1529: 
  1530: \begin{proposition}[Population complete-law reconstruction from three inclusions]\label{lem:algorithmic-complete-law-reconstruction-main}
  1531: Work in the stable quotient domain of
  1532: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality}.  The following deterministic
  1533: map reconstructs the complete stationary visible renewal law from the
  1534: population three-inclusion coordinates
  1535: \[
  1536: r=(r_0,r_1,r_2)
  1537: =\bigl(\PP(A_0=1),\PP(A_0=A_1=1),\PP(A_0=A_2=1)\bigr).
  1538: \]
  1539: It is a population reconstruction theorem only; the finite-record projection
  1540: theorem is \Cref{prop:finite-record-complete-law-renewal-consistency-main},
  1541: and numerical truncation guidance is isolated in
  1542: \Cref{rem:complete-law-numerical-implementation-main}.
  1543: 
  1544: \begin{enumerate}
  1545: \item Start from the population three inclusion coordinates
  1546: \[
  1547: r_0=\PP(A_0=1),\qquad
  1548: r_1=\PP(A_0=A_1=1),\qquad
  1549: r_2=\PP(A_0=A_2=1),
  1550: \]
  1551: and retain the triple only when
  1552: \[
  1553: r_0>0,\qquad \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}:=r_1-r_0^2\ne0 .
  1554: \]
  1555: 
  1556: \item Compute the adjacent Palm mass and hidden-tail ratio
  1557: \[
  1558: a=\frac{r_1}{r_0},\qquad
  1559: \lambda=\frac{r_2-r_0^2}{r_1-r_0^2}.
  1560: \]
  1561: Then form the symmetric root coordinates
  1562: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-sigma-main}
  1563: \sigma_1=1-a+\lambda,\qquad
  1564: \sigma_2=r_0(1-\lambda)-a+\lambda .
  1565: \end{equation}
  1566: For the constrained D-MAP population,
  1567: \Cref{thm:toeplitz_image_inverse_duality} gives
  1568: \[
  1569: (\sigma_1,\sigma_2)=(p+s,ps).
  1570: \]
  1571: 
  1572: \item Recover the unordered survival-factor pair
  1573: \[
  1574: \{z_1,z_2\}
  1575: =\{ \text{roots of } z^2-\sigma_1z+\sigma_2=0\}.
  1576: \]
  1577: Only this unordered pair is a visible-law object.  At the population
  1578: constrained D-MAP point, \(\sigma\) lies in the feasible set \(F\) equivalently
  1579: characterized in \eqref{eq:feasible-symmetric-coordinate-set-main}.
  1580: 
  1581: \item Recover the Palm zero-run gap law.  Put
  1582: \(\theta_i=-\log z_i\).  If \(z_1\ne z_2\), set
  1583: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-gap-main}
  1584: g_k=
  1585: \frac{\theta_2(1-z_1)z_1^k-\theta_1(1-z_2)z_2^k}
  1586:      {\theta_2-\theta_1},
  1587: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1588: \end{equation}
  1589: If \(z_1=z_2=z\), put \(\theta=-\log z\) and use the continuous-limit law
  1590: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-gap-diagonal-main}
  1591: g_k=z^k\bigl((1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\bigr),
  1592: \qquad k\ge0 .
  1593: \end{equation}
  1594: For population roots this is the D-MAP Palm gap law.  For any later
  1595: finite-record projected root pair retained in \((0,1)^2\), the same formulas
  1596: are certified as a nonnegative normalized gap law by
  1597: \Cref{lem:projected-root-gap-law-valid-main}, independently of the noisy
  1598: empirical intensity used to construct the projected coordinate.
  1599: 
  1600: \item Compute the Palm renewal masses
  1601: \[
  1602: u_0=1,\qquad
  1603: u_n=\PP(n\in\varXi\mid0\in\varXi)
  1604:     =\sum_{j=1}^{n}\!
  1605:       \sum_{\substack{k_1,\ldots,k_j\ge0\\
  1606:       (k_1+1)+\cdots+(k_j+1)=n}}
  1607:       g_{k_1}\cdots g_{k_j},
  1608: \qquad n\ge1 .
  1609: \]
  1610: Equivalently, if \(G\) has law \(g\), then
  1611: \[
  1612: u_n=\sum_{j\ge1}\PP\bigl((G_1+1)+\cdots+(G_j+1)=n\bigr),
  1613: \]
  1614: where the sum is finite because each cycle length \(G_i+1\) is at least
  1615: \(1\).
  1616: Equivalently, with the cycle generating function
  1617: \[
  1618: H(t)=\sum_{k\ge0}g_k t^{k+1},
  1619: \]
  1620: the Palm renewal generating function is
  1621: \begin{equation}\label{eq:renewal-generating-function-complete-law-main}
  1622: U(t):=\sum_{n\ge0}u_n t^n
  1623: =\frac{1}{1-H(t)}
  1624: =\frac{1}{1-\sum_{k\ge0}g_k t^{k+1}} .
  1625: \end{equation}
  1626: Equivalently, the coefficients satisfy the finite recursion
  1627: \begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-window-renewal-recursion-main}
  1628: u_0=1,\qquad
  1629: u_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}g_{\ell-1}u_{n-\ell},
  1630: \qquad n\ge1 .
  1631: \end{equation}
  1632: 
  1633: \item Compute the stationary renewal intensity from the same gap law,
  1634: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-rho-star-main}
  1635: \rho_*(\sigma)
  1636: =\frac{1}{\EE(G+1)}
  1637: =\left(1+\sum_{k\ge0}k\,g_k\right)^{-1}.
  1638: \end{equation}
  1639: At the population input produced by the constrained D-MAP, this value equals
  1640: the original inclusion coordinate \(r_0\).  This equality is a conclusion of
  1641: the population model, not a license to combine a noisy finite-record
  1642: \(\widehat r_0\) with a separately projected root pair.
  1643: 
  1644: \item Apply the renewal inclusion product and then finite
  1645: inclusion--exclusion.  For every nonempty
  1646: \(B=\{i_1<\cdots<i_m\}\),
  1647: \begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithmic-reconstruction-inclusion-main}
  1648: \PP(B\subset\varXi)
  1649: =\rho_*(\sigma)\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}u_{i_{\ell+1}-i_\ell},
  1650: \end{equation}

 succeeded in 497ms:
   700: \[
   701: \|\widehat\Sigma_{r,N}^{\rm tot}-\Sigma_r\|_{\rm op}=O_{\PP}(N^{-1/3})
   702: \]
   703: on an event whose probability tends to one.
   704: \end{paperthm}

   706: \begin{paperthm}[Fixed-setting retained-record quotient delta method]\label{thm:finite-record-quotient-delta-main}
   707: Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~C and suppose the population inverse lies in
   708: the stable quotient chart
   709: \[
   710: r_0>0,\qquad \Delta_{\rm inv}=r_1-r_0^2\ne0 .
   711: \]
   712: For the quotient map \(\Phi\) in Theorem~B,
   713: \[
   714: \sqrt N\{\Phi(\Rinch_N^{\rm cyc})-\Phi(\Rinc)\}
   715: \Rightarrow
   716: \mathcal N(0,\Sigma_\sigma),
   717: \qquad
   718: \Sigma_\sigma=D\Phi(\Rinc)\Sigma_rD\Phi(\Rinc)^\top .
   719: \]
   720: For the constrained D-MAP population, \(\Phi(\Rinc)=(p+s,ps)\).  If
   721: \[
   722: \Delta_\sigma=\Phi_1(\Rinc)^2-4\Phi_2(\Rinc)=(p-s)^2>0,
   723: \]
   724: let \(z_-<z_+\) be the sorted roots and let
   725: \(\widehat z_{-,N}<\widehat z_{+,N}\) be the correspondingly sorted roots
   726: formed from \(\Phi(\Rinch_N^{\rm cyc})\).  This ordering is visible and
   727: requires no external physical label.  With
   728: \[
   729: J_{\rm root}=
   730: \begin{pmatrix}
   731: \dfrac{z_-}{z_--z_+}&-\dfrac1{z_--z_+}\\[6pt]
   732: \dfrac{z_+}{z_+-z_-}&-\dfrac1{z_+-z_-}
   733: \end{pmatrix},
   734: \]
   735: the label-free root CLT is
   736: \[
   737: \sqrt N
   738: \begin{pmatrix}\widehat z_{-,N}-z_-\\ \widehat z_{+,N}-z_+\end{pmatrix}
   739: \Rightarrow
   740: \mathcal N\!\left(0,J_{\rm root}\Sigma_\sigma J_{\rm root}^{\top}\right).
   741: \]
   742: For known design interval \(\Delta\tau\), define the root-ordered rates
   743: \[
   744: \vartheta=
   745: \begin{pmatrix}-\log z_-/\Delta\tau\\-\log z_+/\Delta\tau\end{pmatrix},
   746: \qquad
   747: \widehat\vartheta_N=
   748: \begin{pmatrix}-\log\widehat z_{-,N}/\Delta\tau\\
   749: -\log\widehat z_{+,N}/\Delta\tau\end{pmatrix}.
   750: \]
   751: Then
   752: \[
   753: \sqrt N(\widehat\vartheta_N-\vartheta)
   754: \Rightarrow\mathcal N(0,J_{\rm rate}\Sigma_\sigma J_{\rm rate}^{\top}),
   755: \qquad
   756: J_{\rm rate}=
   757: \begin{pmatrix}-(\Delta\tau z_-)^{-1}&0\\
   758: 0&-(\Delta\tau z_+)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}J_{\rm root}.
   759: \]
   760: Thus the roots identify the unordered survival-factor set \(\{p,s\}\) and
   761: the displayed root ordering gives a canonical, label-free version of the
   762: unordered numerical rate set
   763: \[
   764: \left\{-\frac{\log p}{\Delta\tau},-\frac{\log s}{\Delta\tau}\right\}.
   765: \]
   766: If \(\Delta\tau\) is not supplied as a known design interval, the theorem
   767: identifies only the dimensionless unordered product set
   768: \[
   769: \{-\log p,-\log s\}
   770: =\{\Gamma\Delta\tau,\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\},
   771: \]
   772: not numerical rates per unit time.
   773: Only the assignment of the two numerical entries to the physical names
   774: \(\Gamma\) and \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\) requires the side-data branch
   775: convention.
   776: \end{paperthm}
   777: 
   778: \begin{paperthm}[Pointwise exchange-diagonal projection example]\label{thm:e-fixed-candidate-exchange-diagonal-main}
   779: Fix
   780: \[
   781: z_0=\frac12,\qquad \sigma_0=(1,1/4),\qquad
   782: H_{z_0}=\{h\in\mathbb R^2:h_2\le z_0h_1\},
   783: \]
   784: and let \(F=\{(\sigma_1,\sigma_2):\sigma_2\le\sigma_1^2/4\}\).  Suppose the
   785: true population satisfies \(\sigma=\sigma_0\), the printed interval
   786: certificate proves
   787: \(\lambda_{\min}\{\Sigma_\sigma(z_0)\}>0.037\), and
   788: \[
   789: \sqrt N(\widehat\sigma_N^{\rm raw}-\sigma_0)
   790: \Rightarrow Z\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma_\sigma(z_0)).
   791: \]
   792: Let \(\Pi_F\) be the locally unique Euclidean projection, with any fixed
   793: measurable tie rule outside the local neighbourhood, and set
   794: \[
   795: \widehat\sigma_N=\Pi_F(\widehat\sigma_N^{\rm raw}),\qquad
   796: X_N=\sqrt N(\widehat\sigma_N-\sigma_0).
   797: \]
   798: Then
   799: \[
   800: X_N\Rightarrow \Pi_{H_{z_0}}Z .
   801: \]
   802: This theorem is only a pointwise projection example at the preselected point
   803: \(z_0=1/2\).  It defines no statistic for testing, no simulated or limiting
   804: critical value, no confidence region, and no feasible or oracle coverage
   805: claim.  Selection of \(z_0\) from the data and uniformity over \(0<z<1\) are
   806: outside its scope.
   807: \end{paperthm}
   808: 
   809: \begin{proof}
   810: Near \(\sigma_0\), the boundary of \(F\) is the smooth graph
   811: \(\sigma_2=\sigma_1^2/4\), whose tangent cone at \(\sigma_0\) is
   812: \(H_{z_0}\).  The standard first-order metric-projection expansion at a smooth
   813: inequality boundary is
   814: \[
   815: \sqrt N\{\Pi_F(\sigma_0+N^{-1/2}h_N)-\sigma_0\}
   816: =\Pi_{H_{z_0}}h_N+o(1)
   817: \]
   818: for every bounded convergent sequence \(h_N\)
   819: \cite{Shapiro1987MetricProjection}.  Tightness of the raw quotient CLT permits
   820: the expansion on events with probability tending to one.  Slutsky's theorem
   821: and continuity of projection onto the closed half-space give the asserted
   822: limit.  The certificate establishes only that the covariance in this limit is
   823: nonsingular; it is not used to calibrate a procedure.
   824: \end{proof}
   825: 
   826: \subsection{Proofs of Theorems A--D}\label{subsec:proofs-a-d-main}
   827: 
   828: \begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~A]
   829: Write \(\beta=e_D^\top\) and
   830: \(c=((1-p),a)^\top\).  The marked kernel has rank-one reset form
   831: \(T_1=c\beta\).  Conditional on every click, the next hidden state is therefore
   832: \(D\), independently of the pre-click state.  Successive click epochs are
   833: regeneration epochs, and the numbers of intervening zeros are independent with
   834: common Palm mass
   835: \[
   836: g_k=\beta T_0^kT_1\mathbf1,\qquad k\ge0.
   837: \]
   838: The entries of \(T_0\) are strictly positive along every admissible serial path,
   839: so \(g_k>0\) for all \(k\); the spectral radius of \(T_0\) is less than one,
   840: and \(\sum_{k\ge0}g_k=1\).  This proves the full-support renewal assertion.
   841: 
   842: Since \(T_0\) is lower triangular, direct evaluation of \(T_0^k\), followed by
   843: substitution of the integral defining \(b\), gives, off the equal-rate diagonal,
   844: \[
   845: g_k=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-
   846: \Gamma(1-s)s^k}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},\qquad
   847: S_k=\sum_{j\ge k}g_j=
   848: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k}
   849: {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   850: \]
   851: The continuous limit at \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\) follows either by
   852: l'H\^opital's rule or by exponentiating the repeated-eigenvalue triangular
   853: matrix.  Because \(h_k=1-S_{k+1}/S_k\), elementary cancellation yields
   854: \[
   855: h_{k+1}-h_k=
   856: \frac{\Gamma\kappa_{\mathrm r}(p-s)^2p^ks^k}
   857: {(\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k)
   858:  (\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^{k+1}-\Gamma s^{k+1})}>0.
   859: \]
   860: On the diagonal, with \(y=\Gamma\Delta\tau\) and \(p=e^{-y}\), the same

 succeeded in 532ms:
   120: removable equal-rate branch \(x=y\) is explicitly excluded.  Only at \(y=1\)
   121: does the equal-rate branch become a genuine zero, giving
   122: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=\kappa_{\mathrm r}=1/\Delta\tau\).
   123: \end{lemma}
   124: 
   125: \begin{proof}
   126: The identity \(p-b\) and the quotient form follow from
   127: \eqref{eq:a-b-main}.  The equal-rate value is l'Hopital's rule, equivalently
   128: \(b=y e^{-y}\) when \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}=\Gamma\), and hence
   129: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=e^{-y}(1-y)\).  For \(x\ne y\), multiplying the
   130: quotient numerator by \(\Delta\tau\) gives the scalar equation
   131: \(x e^{-x}=y e^{-y}\).  The function \(x\mapsto xe^{-x}\) is strictly
   132: increasing on \((0,1)\), strictly decreasing on \((1,\infty)\), and has its
   133: only maximum at \(1\).  Hence the level through \(y\ne1\) has exactly one
   134: positive solution on the opposite side of \(1\), in addition to the removable
   135: solution \(x=y\).  This gives the two non-removable branches in
   136: \eqref{eq:gamma-crit-piecewise-main}.  When \(y=1\), the opposite branch
   137: coalesces with the diagonal and the equal-rate limit \(e^{-1}(1-1)=0\) gives
   138: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=1/\Delta\tau=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\).
   139: \end{proof}
   140: 
   141: For later audits, the equal-rate diagonal
   142: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), \(y=\Gamma\Delta\tau\), \(p=e^{-y}\), is
   143: summarized by the single dictionary
   144: \begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}
   145: \begin{aligned}
   146: a&=1-(1+y)p,&
   147: b&=yp,\\
   148: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}&=p-b=(1-y)p,&
   149: S_k&=\PP(G\ge k)=p^k(1+yk),\\
   150: g_k&=p^k\bigl((1-p)(1+yk)-yp\bigr),&
   151: h_k&=\frac{(1-p)+y(k(1-p)-p)}{1+yk},
   152: \qquad k\ge0 .
   153: \end{aligned}
   154: \end{equation}
   155: 
   156: \begin{lemma}[Regeneration and zero-run law]\label{lem:renewal-gap-main}
   157: Let \(G\) be the number of zeros between consecutive clicks in the stationary
   158: constrained D-MAP record.  Click times are regeneration times.  For
   159: \(k\ge0\),
   160: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-main}
   161: g_k=\PP(G=k)=e_D^\top T_0^kT_1\mathbf1 .
   162: \end{equation}
   163: If \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\ne\Gamma\), then
   164: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap-closed-main}
   165: g_k=
   166: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   167:      {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   168: \end{equation}
   169: The gap law has full support on \(\mathbb N_0\) and an exponential tail.
   170: \end{lemma}
   171: 
   172: \begin{proof}
   173: After a click the next hidden state is \(D\), independently of the earlier
   174: record.  Thus a gap of \(k\) zeros followed by a click has probability
   175: \eqref{eq:gap-main}.  Since \(T_0\) is lower triangular,
   176: \[
   177: \textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}=0\quad(k=0),
   178: \]
   179: with the displayed sum interpreted as empty when \(k=0\), and
   180: \[
   181: T_0^k=
   182: \begin{pmatrix}
   183: p^k&0\\
   184: b\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}&s^k
   185: \end{pmatrix}.
   186: \]
   187: Therefore
   188: \[
   189: e_D^\top T_0^kT_1\mathbf1
   190: =b(1-p)\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}+as^k,
   191: \]
   192: where the comma only separates the display from the following substitution.
   193: For \(p\ne s\),
   194: \[
   195: \sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^jp^{k-1-j}=\frac{p^k-s^k}{p-s}.
   196: \]
   197: Substituting
   198: \[
   199: a=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)-\Gamma(1-s)}
   200:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   201: \qquad
   202: b=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(p-s)}
   203:         {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
   204: \]
   205: therefore gives
   206: \[
   207: b(1-p)\frac{p^k-s^k}{p-s}+as^k
   208: =
   209: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-\Gamma(1-s)s^k}
   210:      {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},
   211: \]
   212: which is \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main}.  If \(p=s\), equivalently
   213: \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}=\Gamma\), the same identity is read by the continuous
   214: equal-rate dictionary \eqref{eq:equal-rate-dictionary-main}.  All terms are
   215: probabilities of disjoint paths, so they are positive, and the two survival
   216: factors lie in \((0,1)\).
   217: \end{proof}
   218: 
   219: \begin{corollary}[Support of finite binary words]\label{cor:word-support-main}
   220: Every finite binary word has positive probability under the stationary
   221: constrained D-MAP record.
   222: \end{corollary}
   223: 
   224: \begin{proof}
   225: Let the word be \(w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-1})\), with \(m\ge1\), and write
   226: \[
   227: S=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}:w_i=1\}.
   228: \]
   229: We work in the two-sided stationary renewal click set
   230: \(\varXi=\{n:A_n=1\}\).  Embed the observed window in the longer interval
   231: \([-1,m]\) and force clicks at both artificial endpoints.  More explicitly,
   232: list the finite set
   233: \[
   234: C=\{-1\}\cup S\cup\{m\}=\{c_0<c_1<\cdots<c_J\}.
   235: \]
   236: Consider the event that \(c_0,c_1,\ldots,c_J\) are consecutive renewal
   237: clicks, with no further clicks between them.  Under the stationary law this
   238: event has probability
   239: \[
   240: \PP(c_0\in\varXi)\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}
   241: \PP\bigl(G=c_{j+1}-c_j-1\bigr)
   242: =\rho\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}g_{c_{j+1}-c_j-1}.
   243: \]
   244: Each displayed gap length is a nonnegative integer, including at the two
   245: observed-window boundaries.  By \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main},
   246: \(\rho>0\) and \(g_k>0\) for every \(k\ge0\), so the event has positive
   247: probability.  On this event the restriction of the click set to
   248: \(\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\) is exactly \(S\): the forced interior clicks are precisely
   249: the one-sites of \(w\), and the ``consecutive clicks'' condition rules out
   250: clicks at every zero-site.  Hence the cylinder probability of \(w\) is at
   251: least this positive probability.  The empty word has probability \(1\).
   252: \end{proof}
   253: 
   254: \subsection{Exact dependence, hazard, and count diagnostics}
   255: 
   256: These are falsification diagnostics only; no size, power, or post-selection
   257: coverage theorem is proved for these diagnostics.
   258: The hazard, Fano, and low-lag formulas in this subsection have the inferential
   259: status fixed in \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}.  The hazard,
   260: spectral-density, and Fano displays in this subsection are population
   261: identities or inputs to later sandwich/projection procedures.
   262: 
   263: \begin{lemma}[Stationary hidden law]\label{lem:stationary-main}
   264: For \(P=T_0+T_1=\begin{psmallmatrix}p&q\\ b&1-b\end{psmallmatrix}\), the
   265: stationary hidden law is
   266: \[
   267: \pi=\left(\frac b{q+b},\frac{q}{q+b}\right).
   268: \]
   269: With \(q=1-p\) as fixed in \Cref{lem:equal-rate-critical-convention-main}, the
   270: stationary click density is
   271: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rho-main}
   272: \rho=\pi T_1\mathbf1=\frac{q(1-s)}{q+b}.
   273: \end{equation}
   274: Moreover \(a<q(1-s)<q\), and therefore
   275: \(a-\rho=-b(q-a)/(q+b)<0\).
   276: \end{lemma}
   277: 
   278: \begin{proof}
   279: Solving \(\pi P=\pi\) gives the displayed law.  Since
   280: \(u=T_1\mathbf1=(q,a)^\top\) and \(a+b=1-s\), \(\rho=\pi u\) gives
   281: \eqref{eq:rho-main}.  For \(r>0\),
   282: \[
   283: 1-e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}<1-e^{-\Gamma\Delta\tau}=q,
   284: \]
   285: so the defining integral for \(a\) gives
   286: \[
   287: a<q\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}\,dr
   288: =q(1-s)<q .
   289: \]
   290: The sign follows from
   291: \[
   292: a-\rho=-\frac{b(q-a)}{q+b}
   293: \]
   294: and the strict positivity of \(b\) and \(q-a\).
   295: \end{proof}
   296: 
   297: \begin{lemma}[Covariance transfer through the hidden mode]\label{lem:covariance-transfer-main}
   298: For the stationary D-MAP record,
   299: \begin{equation}\label{eq:cov-transfer-main}
   300: \PP(A_n=1\mid A_0=1)
   301: =\rho+(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1},
   302: \qquad
   303: \operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_n)=\rho(a-\rho)\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}
   304: \end{equation}
   305: for \(n\ge1\).
   306: \end{lemma}
   307: 
   308: \begin{proof}
   309: With \(q=1-p\) and \(u=(q,a)^\top\),
   310: \[
   311: u-\rho\mathbf1=\frac{q-a}{q+b}\binom {q}{-b},
   312: \qquad
   313: P\binom {q}{-b}=(p-b)\binom {q}{-b}.
   314: \]
   315: Thus
   316: \[
   317: P^{n-1}u=\rho\mathbf1+
   318: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{n-1}(u-\rho\mathbf1).
   319: \]
   320: A click at time \(0\) sets the next hidden state to \(D\).  Hence
   321: \[
   322: \PP(A_n=1\mid A_0=1)
   323: =e_D^\top P^{n-1}u
   324: =\rho+\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{n-1}(a-\rho),
   325: \]
   326: because the \(D\)-component of \(u-\rho\mathbf1\) is \(a-\rho\).  Multiplying
   327: by \(\rho=\PP(A_0=1)\) and subtracting \(\rho^2\) gives the covariance in
   328: \eqref{eq:cov-transfer-main}.
   329: \end{proof}
   330: 
   331: \begin{lemma}[Critical-rate uniqueness and covariance phase]\label{lem:critical-rate-main}
   332: For fixed \(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau>0\), the equation
   333: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}(\Gamma)=0\) has the unique positive solution
   334: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}(\kappa_{\mathrm r},\Delta\tau)\) defined by the
   335: piecewise non-removable branch
   336: \eqref{eq:gamma-crit-piecewise-main}.  When
   337: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}\ne\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), this is equivalently
   338: \begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma-c-main}
   339: \Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}\Delta\tau}
   340: =\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau}.
   341: \end{equation}
   342: The displayed scalar equation also has the diagonal solution
   343: \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{crit}}=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), but that solution is removable
   344: for \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\) unless
   345: \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau=1\).  Thus on the diagonal
   346: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\), the zero occurs only at
   347: \(\Gamma=\kappa_{\mathrm r}=1/\Delta\tau\).
   348: The sign of \(\operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_n)\) is the sign of
   349: \(-\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}^{\,n-1}\).
   350: \end{lemma}
   351: 
   352: \begin{proof}
   353: The uniqueness is the branch statement in
   354: \Cref{lem:equal-rate-critical-convention-main}.  The covariance sign follows
   355: from \(\rho>0\) and \(a-\rho<0\) in \Cref{lem:stationary-main}.
   356: \end{proof}
   357: 
   358: \begin{lemma}[Hidden-mode contraction]\label{lem:hidden-mode-contraction-main}
   359: For every positive constrained D-MAP setting,
   360: \[
   361: |\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1 .
   362: \]
   363: In particular the shifted geometric Fourier series in
   364: \Cref{prop:spectrum-main} is absolutely convergent.
   365: \end{lemma}
   366: 
   367: \begin{proof}
   368: The transition matrix
   369: \[
   370: P=T_0+T_1=
   371: \begin{pmatrix}
   372: p&1-p\\ b&1-b
   373: \end{pmatrix}
   374: \]
   375: is a two-state stochastic matrix with strictly positive off-diagonal entries
   376: \(1-p>0\) and \(b>0\).  Its eigenvalues are \(1\) and
   377: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\).  The upper bound is immediate from \(p<1\)
   378: and \(b>0\): \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}<1\).  For the lower bound, \(p>0\) and
   379: \[
   380: b=\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}
   381: e^{-\Gamma(\Delta\tau-r)}\,dr
   382: <\int_0^{\Delta\tau}\kappa_{\mathrm r}e^{-\kappa_{\mathrm r}r}\,dr
   383: =1-s<1 .
   384: \]
   385: Thus \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b>-1\).  Hence
   386: \(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1\).  Absolute convergence follows from
   387: \(\sum_{n\ge1}|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|^{n-1}<\infty\).
   388: \end{proof}
   389: 
   390: \begin{lemma}[Finite hidden-start path-space contraction]\label{lem:finite-hidden-start-path-contraction-main}
   391: Fix one positive constrained D-MAP setting and put \(P=T_0+T_1\).  Let
   392: \(\pi\) be the stationary hidden law.  For a hidden initial law \(\nu\), let
   393: \(\PP_\nu\) denote the one-sided hidden/visible law started from \(H_0\sim\nu\),
   394: and let \(\PP_\pi\) denote the stationary-start law.  There are constants
   395: \(C<\infty\) and \(\theta\in(0,1)\), depending only on the fixed setting, such
   396: that for every burn-in length \(L\ge0\), every retained length \(N\ge1\), and
   397: every set \(B\subset\{0,1\}^N\),
   398: \begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-hidden-start-path-tv-main}
   399: \left|
   400: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   401: -
   402: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   403: \right|
   404: \le C\theta^L .
   405: \end{equation}
   406: Equivalently,
   407: \[
   408: \sup_{B\subset\{0,1\}^N}
   409: \left|
   410: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   411: -
   412: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   413: \right|
   414: \le C\theta^L ,
   415: \]
   416: up to the harmless convention-dependent factor in the definition of total
   417: variation.  The bound is uniform in \(N\).  Consequently, if \(L_N\to\infty\)
   418: and \(S_N\) is any measurable statistic of the retained visible word
   419: \((A_{L_N},\ldots,A_{L_N+N-1})\), then every stationary-start convergence in
   420: distribution, convergence in probability, or event-probability limit for
   421: \(S_N\) transfers unchanged to the finite hidden start.
   422: \end{lemma}
   423: 
   424: \begin{proof}
   425: For a word \(w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{N-1})\), write
   426: \(T_w=T_{w_0}\cdots T_{w_{N-1}}\).  For a set
   427: \(B\subset\{0,1\}^N\), put \(M_B=\sum_{w\in B}T_w\).  Since
   428: \(\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^N}T_w=P^N\), the matrix \(M_B\) is substochastic: its
   429: entries are nonnegative and \(0\le M_B\mathbf1\le\mathbf1\) coordinatewise.
   430: The retained visible word probability from the finite start is therefore
   431: \[
   432: \PP_\nu\bigl((A_L,\ldots,A_{L+N-1})\in B\bigr)
   433: =\nu P^L M_B\mathbf1,
   434: \]
   435: while stationarity gives
   436: \[
   437: \PP_\pi\bigl((A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1})\in B\bigr)
   438: =\pi M_B\mathbf1 .
   439: \]
   440: Thus the difference is
   441: \[
   442: (\nu P^L-\pi)M_B\mathbf1 .
   443: \]
   444: Because \(0\le M_B\mathbf1\le\mathbf1\), the total-variation dual inequality
   445: gives
   446: \[
   447: \left|(\nu P^L-\pi)M_B\mathbf1\right|
   448: \le \|\nu P^L-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}} .
   449: \]
   450: 
   451: It remains only to bound the hidden-chain term.  The matrix
   452: \[
   453: P=\begin{pmatrix}p&1-p\\ b&1-b\end{pmatrix}
   454: \]
   455: has strictly positive off-diagonal entries, stationary law
   456: \(\pi=(b/(1-p+b),(1-p)/(1-p+b))\), and nontrivial eigenvalue
   457: \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=p-b\).  By
   458: \Cref{lem:hidden-mode-contraction-main}, \(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|<1\).
   459: On the two-state zero-sum subspace, multiplication by \(P\) is multiplication
   460: by \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\).  Hence, for every initial law \(\nu\),
   461: \[
   462: \|\nu P^L-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
   463: \le C|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|^L
   464: \]
   465: with a finite constant \(C\) uniform over \(\nu\).  Taking any
   466: \(\theta\in(|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|,1)\), or \(\theta=|\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}|\)
   467: when \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\ne0\) and increasing \(C\) if desired, proves
   468: \eqref{eq:finite-hidden-start-path-tv-main}.
   469: 
   470: The final assertion follows because every statistic of the retained word is a
   471: measurable image of that \(N\)-word.  Total variation cannot increase under a
   472: measurable map, so the law of \(S_N\) under the finite hidden start differs
   473: from its stationary-start law by at most \(C\theta^{L_N}\to0\).  Applying this
   474: bound to continuity sets gives distributional convergence; applying it to the
   475: events \(\{|S_N-s|>\varepsilon\}\), or to the events appearing in a stated
   476: probability limit, gives convergence in probability and event-probability
   477: limits.
   478: \end{proof}
   479: 
   480: \begin{lemma}[Hazard obstruction to finite symbolic memory]\label{lem:hazard-obstruction-main}
   481: A renewal binary process with full gap support is a finite-order Markov chain
   482: only if its gap hazard is eventually constant.
   483: \end{lemma}
   484: 
   485: \begin{proof}
   486: For a renewal process, after observing \(k\) consecutive zeros since the last
   487: click, the conditional click probability is the zero-run hazard \(h_k\).
   488: If an \(r\)-step Markov rule generated the same full-support law, then all
   489: histories with \(k\ge r\) trailing zeros would have the same last \(r\) symbols
   490: and hence the same click probability.  Therefore \(h_k\) would be constant for
   491: all \(k\ge r\).
   492: 
   493: This condition concerns a transition rule conditioned on the most recent
   494: symbols.  It is different from finite dependence of separated sigma-fields:
   495: \(1\)-dependence says that events separated by one unobserved sample
   496: factorize, while an \(r\)-step Markov rule says that every future one-step
   497: transition probability is a function of only the last \(r\) symbols.  For a
   498: renewal record with full gap support, the latter would force the click
   499: probability after a long zero run to stop depending on the run length.
   500: \end{proof}
   501: 
   502: \begin{lemma}[Cylinder factorization at the memory threshold]\label{lem:kernel-factorization-main}
   503: If \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), then \(P=T_0+T_1=\mathbf1\pi\).  More
   504: explicitly, let \(u=(u_0,\ldots,u_{r-1})\) and
   505: \(v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{\ell-1})\) be arbitrary finite binary words and write
   506: \(T_u=T_{u_0}\cdots T_{u_{r-1}}\), \(T_v=T_{v_0}\cdots T_{v_{\ell-1}}\).
   507: If the word \(u\) is observed on one block and \(v\) is observed on a second
   508: block beginning two samples later, with the intervening sample unobserved,
   509: then
   510: \[
   511: \PP(A_0^{r-1}=u,\ A_{r+1}^{r+\ell}=v)
   512: =\pi T_u P T_v\mathbf1
   513: =\bigl(\pi T_u\mathbf1\bigr)\bigl(\pi T_v\mathbf1\bigr).
   514: \]
   515: Consequently every two visible blocks separated by one unobserved sample are
   516: independent, so the visible record is exact \(1\)-dependent.
   517: \end{lemma}
   518: 
   519: \begin{proof}
   520: The second eigenvalue of \(P\) is \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\), while the first is
   521: \(1\).  At \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}=0\), the primitive stochastic matrix
   522: \(P\) has rank one and both rows equal \(\pi\), hence \(P=\mathbf1\pi\).
   523: For fixed words \(u\) and \(v\), summing over the unobserved sample between
   524: the two displayed blocks inserts \(T_0+T_1=P\).  The stationary cylinder
   525: probability is therefore
   526: \[
   527: \pi T_u(T_0+T_1)T_v\mathbf1=\pi T_uPT_v\mathbf1.
   528: \]
   529: At the threshold,
   530: \[
   531: \pi T_uPT_v\mathbf1
   532: =\pi T_u\mathbf1\,\pi T_v\mathbf1
   533: =\PP(A_0^{r-1}=u)\PP(A_0^{\ell-1}=v),
   534: \]
   535: where the last equality is stationarity applied to the two single-block
   536: cylinders.  Thus every pair of finite cylinder events supported on two
   537: finite blocks with one omitted sample between them factorizes.  Translating
   538: the indices gives the same statement for any blocks
   539: \(\{i,\ldots,m\}\) and \(\{m+2,\ldots,j\}\).  Since finite cylinders generate
   540: the corresponding finite-block sigma algebras, the two finite-block sigma

 succeeded in 503ms:
   760: Thus the roots identify the unordered survival-factor set \(\{p,s\}\) and
   761: the displayed root ordering gives a canonical, label-free version of the
   762: unordered numerical rate set
   763: \[
   764: \left\{-\frac{\log p}{\Delta\tau},-\frac{\log s}{\Delta\tau}\right\}.
   765: \]
   766: If \(\Delta\tau\) is not supplied as a known design interval, the theorem
   767: identifies only the dimensionless unordered product set
   768: \[
   769: \{-\log p,-\log s\}
   770: =\{\Gamma\Delta\tau,\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau\},
   771: \]
   772: not numerical rates per unit time.
   773: Only the assignment of the two numerical entries to the physical names
   774: \(\Gamma\) and \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\) requires the side-data branch
   775: convention.
   776: \end{paperthm}

   778: \begin{paperthm}[Pointwise exchange-diagonal projection example]\label{thm:e-fixed-candidate-exchange-diagonal-main}
   779: Fix
   780: \[
   781: z_0=\frac12,\qquad \sigma_0=(1,1/4),\qquad
   782: H_{z_0}=\{h\in\mathbb R^2:h_2\le z_0h_1\},
   783: \]
   784: and let \(F=\{(\sigma_1,\sigma_2):\sigma_2\le\sigma_1^2/4\}\).  Suppose the
   785: true population satisfies \(\sigma=\sigma_0\), the printed interval
   786: certificate proves
   787: \(\lambda_{\min}\{\Sigma_\sigma(z_0)\}>0.037\), and
   788: \[
   789: \sqrt N(\widehat\sigma_N^{\rm raw}-\sigma_0)
   790: \Rightarrow Z\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma_\sigma(z_0)).
   791: \]
   792: Let \(\Pi_F\) be the locally unique Euclidean projection, with any fixed
   793: measurable tie rule outside the local neighbourhood, and set
   794: \[
   795: \widehat\sigma_N=\Pi_F(\widehat\sigma_N^{\rm raw}),\qquad
   796: X_N=\sqrt N(\widehat\sigma_N-\sigma_0).
   797: \]
   798: Then
   799: \[
   800: X_N\Rightarrow \Pi_{H_{z_0}}Z .
   801: \]
   802: This theorem is only a pointwise projection example at the preselected point
   803: \(z_0=1/2\).  It defines no statistic for testing, no simulated or limiting
   804: critical value, no confidence region, and no feasible or oracle coverage
   805: claim.  Selection of \(z_0\) from the data and uniformity over \(0<z<1\) are
   806: outside its scope.
   807: \end{paperthm}
   808: 
   809: \begin{proof}
   810: Near \(\sigma_0\), the boundary of \(F\) is the smooth graph
   811: \(\sigma_2=\sigma_1^2/4\), whose tangent cone at \(\sigma_0\) is
   812: \(H_{z_0}\).  The standard first-order metric-projection expansion at a smooth
   813: inequality boundary is
   814: \[
   815: \sqrt N\{\Pi_F(\sigma_0+N^{-1/2}h_N)-\sigma_0\}
   816: =\Pi_{H_{z_0}}h_N+o(1)
   817: \]
   818: for every bounded convergent sequence \(h_N\)
   819: \cite{Shapiro1987MetricProjection}.  Tightness of the raw quotient CLT permits
   820: the expansion on events with probability tending to one.  Slutsky's theorem
   821: and continuity of projection onto the closed half-space give the asserted
   822: limit.  The certificate establishes only that the covariance in this limit is
   823: nonsingular; it is not used to calibrate a procedure.
   824: \end{proof}
   825: 
   826: \subsection{Proofs of Theorems A--D}\label{subsec:proofs-a-d-main}
   827: 
   828: \begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~A]
   829: Write \(\beta=e_D^\top\) and
   830: \(c=((1-p),a)^\top\).  The marked kernel has rank-one reset form
   831: \(T_1=c\beta\).  Conditional on every click, the next hidden state is therefore
   832: \(D\), independently of the pre-click state.  Successive click epochs are
   833: regeneration epochs, and the numbers of intervening zeros are independent with
   834: common Palm mass
   835: \[
   836: g_k=\beta T_0^kT_1\mathbf1,\qquad k\ge0.
   837: \]
   838: The entries of \(T_0\) are strictly positive along every admissible serial path,
   839: so \(g_k>0\) for all \(k\); the spectral radius of \(T_0\) is less than one,
   840: and \(\sum_{k\ge0}g_k=1\).  This proves the full-support renewal assertion.
   841: 
   842: Since \(T_0\) is lower triangular, direct evaluation of \(T_0^k\), followed by
   843: substitution of the integral defining \(b\), gives, off the equal-rate diagonal,
   844: \[
   845: g_k=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}(1-p)p^k-
   846: \Gamma(1-s)s^k}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma},\qquad
   847: S_k=\sum_{j\ge k}g_j=
   848: \frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}p^k-\Gamma s^k}
   849: {\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}.
   850: \]

  2901: eigenvalue falls below \(t_N\), with probability tending to one whenever

  2903: =o_{\PP}(t_N)\).

  2906: 
  2907: \begin{lemma}[Conditional projected-Gaussian law at a moving half-space]\label{lem:projected-gaussian-bootstrap-halfspace-main}
  2908: For \(t\in(0,1)\), write
  2909: \[
  2910: H_t=\{h\in\RR^2:h_2\le th_1\}.
  2911: \]
  2912: Let \(z_N\to z\in(0,1)\) in probability and let
  2913: \(\widehat\Sigma_N\) be a symmetric nonnegative definite covariance estimator
  2914: such that
  2915: \[
  2916: \|\widehat\Sigma_N-\Sigma\|_{\operatorname{op}}=o_{\PP}(t_N),
  2917: \qquad t_N\downarrow0,
  2918: \]
  2919: for a fixed nonnegative definite \(2\times2\) matrix \(\Sigma\).  Let
  2920: \[
  2921: \widehat\Sigma_N^+
  2922: =\sum_{\widehat\lambda_{i,N}>t_N}
  2923: \widehat\lambda_{i,N}^{-1}
  2924: \widehat v_{i,N}\widehat v_{i,N}^{\top}
  2925: \]
  2926: be the thresholded Moore--Penrose inverse.  Suppose that
  2927: \[
  2928: X_N\Rightarrow X:=\Pi_{H_z}(Z),
  2929: \qquad Z\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma).
  2930: \]
  2931: This is the local constrained-estimation limit: the Gaussian perturbation is
  2932: projected onto the tangent half-space before the quadratic statistic is formed.
  2933: It is the same projected-normal structure that appears in constrained
  2934: \(M\)-estimation limits \cite{Geyer1994ConstrainedM}.  The simulation below is
  2935: therefore not an ordinary nonparametric or parametric bootstrap from the
  2936: record; it is the conditional plug-in projected Gaussian law, with retained
  2937: empirical rank.
  2938: Define
  2939: \[
  2940: T_N=X_N^\top\widehat\Sigma_N^+X_N
  2941: \]
  2942: and, conditionally on the observed record,
  2943: \[
  2944: Z_N^\ast\sim \mathcal N(0,\widehat\Sigma_N),\qquad
  2945: X_N^\ast=\Pi_{H_{z_N}}(Z_N^\ast),\qquad
  2946: T_N^\ast=(X_N^\ast)^\top\widehat\Sigma_N^+X_N^\ast .
  2947: \]
  2948: Then
  2949: \[
  2950: T_N\Rightarrow T:=X^\top\Sigma^+X,
  2951: \]
  2952: and the conditional law of \(T_N^\ast\) converges weakly in probability to
  2953: the law of \(T\).  Consequently, if \(c_{1-\alpha,N}^{\ast,-}\) is the lower
  2954: conditional \((1-\alpha)\)-quantile of \(T_N^\ast\) and the limiting quantile
  2955: \[
  2956: c_{1-\alpha}:=\inf\{c:\PP(T\le c)\ge1-\alpha\}
  2957: \]
  2958: is a continuity point of the distribution function of \(T\), then
  2959: \[
  2960: c_{1-\alpha,N}^{\ast,-}\to c_{1-\alpha}
  2961: \quad\text{in probability}.
  2962: \]
  2963: If in addition \(\PP(T\le c_{1-\alpha})=1-\alpha\), then
  2964: \[
  2965: \PP\{T_N\le c_{1-\alpha,N}^{\ast,-}\}\to1-\alpha .
  2966: \]
  2967: If \(\Sigma=0\), then the retained-rank inverse is the zero matrix with
  2968: probability tending to one; consequently \(T_N=0\) and \(T_N^\ast=0\) with
  2969: probability tending to one, and the fixed positive-buffer lower-coverage
  2970: conclusion follows from
  2971: \Cref{lem:buffered-random-quantile-coverage-main}.
  2972: 
  2973: \end{lemma}
  2974: 
  2975: \begin{proof}
  2976: Weyl's inequality and the condition
  2977: \(\|\widehat\Sigma_N-\Sigma\|_{\operatorname{op}}=o_{\PP}(t_N)\) imply that,
  2978: with probability tending to one, every positive eigenvalue of \(\Sigma\) is
  2979: retained and every zero eigenvalue is removed by the threshold.  On that event
  2980: the retained spectral projection converges to the range projection of
  2981: \(\Sigma\), and
  2982: \[
  2983: \|\widehat\Sigma_N^+-\Sigma^+\|_{\operatorname{op}}\to0
  2984: \]
  2985: in probability.  Slutsky's theorem therefore gives
  2986: \[
  2987: X_N^\top\widehat\Sigma_N^+X_N
  2988: \Rightarrow X^\top\Sigma^+X .
  2989: \]
  2990: In the special case \(\Sigma=0\), the same eigenvalue bound says
  2991: \[
  2992: \PP\{\|\widehat\Sigma_N\|_{\operatorname{op}}\le t_N\}\to1,
  2993: \]
  2994: so the thresholded retained-rank inverse is exactly zero with probability
  2995: tending to one.  Hence \(T_N=0\) and, conditionally, \(T_N^\ast=0\) on that
  2996: high-probability event.  The limiting distribution is the point mass at zero,
  2997: whose lower \(q\)-quantile is zero for every \(0<q<1\); the positive-buffer
  2998: coverage statement is therefore the buffered random-quantile conclusion.
  2999: 
  3000: The remaining issue is conditional Gaussian convergence after the moving
  3001: projection.  The half-space projection has the explicit formula
  3002: \[
  3003: \Pi_{H_t}(u)
  3004: =u-\frac{(u_2-tu_1)_+}{1+t^2}\binom{-t}{1}.
  3005: \]
  3006: Thus \((t,u)\mapsto\Pi_{H_t}(u)\) is continuous, uniformly for \(t\) in compact
  3007: subintervals of \((0,1)\) and \(u\) in bounded sets.  Since
  3008: \(\widehat\Sigma_N\to\Sigma\) in probability, the conditional Gaussian laws
  3009: \(\mathcal N(0,\widehat\Sigma_N)\) converge weakly in probability to
  3010: \(\mathcal N(0,\Sigma)\).  Combining this Gaussian continuity with
  3011: \(z_N\to z\) and \(\widehat\Sigma_N^+\to\Sigma^+\) shows that, for every
  3012: bounded continuous \(\varphi:\RR\to\RR\),
  3013: \[
  3014: \EE^\ast\varphi(T_N^\ast)
  3015: \to
  3016: \EE\varphi\!\left((\Pi_{H_z}Z)^\top\Sigma^+\Pi_{H_z}Z\right)
  3017: \]
  3018: in probability, where \(\EE^\ast\) denotes conditional expectation given the
  3019: record.  This is conditional weak convergence in probability.  The quantile
  3020: conclusion is the standard consequence of weak convergence at a continuity
  3021: point of the limiting distribution function; the final coverage statement
  3022: then follows from the convergence of \(T_N\) and the random quantile
  3023: \(c_{1-\alpha,N}^{\ast,-}\).
  3024: \end{proof}
  3025: 
  3026: \begin{lemma}[Buffered random-quantile lower coverage]\label{lem:buffered-random-quantile-coverage-main}
  3027: Let \(T_N\Rightarrow T\), and let \(F_N^\ast\) be random conditional
  3028: distribution functions such that
  3029: \[
  3030: F_N^\ast(t)\to F(t):=\PP(T\le t)
  3031: \qquad\text{in probability}
  3032: \]
  3033: at every continuity point \(t\) of \(F\).  Fix \(0<q<1\), and define the
  3034: lower random conditional \(q\)-quantile and the limiting lower \(q\)-quantile
  3035: by
  3036: \[
  3037: c_N^-=\inf\{t:F_N^\ast(t)\ge q\},
  3038: \qquad
  3039: c^-=\inf\{t:F(t)\ge q\}.
  3040: \]
  3041: Assume \(c^-\) is finite.  Then, for every fixed \(\varepsilon>0\),
  3042: \[
  3043: \liminf_{N\to\infty}\PP\{T_N\le c_N^-+\varepsilon\}\ge q .
  3044: \]
  3045: No continuity of \(F\) at \(c^-\), and no equality \(F(c^-)=q\), is required.
  3046: In particular, this lemma applies to the diagonal projected-Gaussian
  3047: zero-quantile alternatives after replacing \(q\) by \(1-\alpha\).
  3048: \end{lemma}
  3049: 
  3050: \begin{proof}
  3051: First the random lower quantile cannot fall a fixed distance below the
  3052: limiting lower quantile.  Fix \(\delta>0\).  By the definition of \(c^-\),
  3053: \(F(c^--\delta)<q\).  Choose a continuity point
  3054: \[
  3055: x\in(c^--\delta,c^-)
  3056: \]
  3057: with \(F(x)<q\); distribution functions have at most countably many
  3058: discontinuities, so such a point is available after decreasing \(x\) slightly
  3059: if needed.  The pointwise conditional convergence gives
  3060: \[
  3061: F_N^\ast(x)\to F(x)
  3062: \qquad\text{in probability}.
  3063: \]
  3064: For any distribution function \(G\), the event
  3065: \(\inf\{t:G(t)\ge q\}\le x\) implies \(G(x)\ge q\).  Hence
  3066: \[
  3067: \PP\{c_N^-\le x\}
  3068: \le
  3069: \PP\{F_N^\ast(x)\ge q\}\to0,
  3070: \]
  3071: and \(c_N^->x\) with probability tending to one.
  3072: 
  3073: Choose \(x>c^- -\varepsilon\), and then choose a continuity point
  3074: \[
  3075: y\in(c^-,x+\varepsilon).
  3076: \]
  3077: This is possible because \(x+\varepsilon>c^-\).  On the event
  3078: \(\{c_N^->x\}\), the inclusion
  3079: \[
  3080: \{T_N\le y\}\subseteq\{T_N\le c_N^-+\varepsilon\}
  3081: \]
  3082: holds.  Therefore
  3083: \[
  3084: \liminf_N\PP\{T_N\le c_N^-+\varepsilon\}
  3085: \ge
  3086: \liminf_N\PP\{T_N\le y\}.
  3087: \]
  3088: Weak convergence at the continuity point \(y\) gives
  3089: \[
  3090: \lim_N\PP\{T_N\le y\}=F(y).
  3091: \]
  3092: Finally \(y>c^-\) implies \(F(y)\ge q\) by the lower-quantile definition.
  3093: The displayed lower bound follows.
  3094: \end{proof}
  3095: 
  3096: \begin{lemma}[Conditional Monte Carlo diagnostic and auxiliary domination]\label{lem:conditional-monte-carlo-quantile-main}
  3097: Fix \(0<z<1\) and condition on the observed record.  Let
  3098: \[
  3099: T_N^\ast(z)=
  3100: \Pi_{H_z}(Z_N^\ast)^\top\widehat\Sigma_{\sigma,N}^{+}
  3101: \Pi_{H_z}(Z_N^\ast),
  3102: \qquad
  3103: Z_N^\ast\mid A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1}\sim
  3104: \mathcal N(0,\widehat\Sigma_{\sigma,N}),
  3105: \]
  3106: where \(H_z=\{h:h_2\le zh_1\}\), and let
  3107: \[
  3108: F_N^\ast(t;z)=\PP^\ast\{T_N^\ast(z)\le t\}
  3109: \]
  3110: be its conditional distribution function.  If
  3111: \(T_{N,1}^\ast(z),\ldots,T_{N,R}^\ast(z)\) are conditionally independent draws
  3112: from this law and
  3113: \[
  3114: \widehat F_{N,R}^\ast(t;z)
  3115: =\frac1R\sum_{m=1}^R\mathbf1_{\{T_{N,m}^\ast(z)\le t\}},
  3116: \]
  3117: then for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
  3118: \[
  3119: \PP^\ast\!\left(
  3120: \sup_{t\in\mathbb R}
  3121: \left|\widehat F_{N,R}^\ast(t;z)-F_N^\ast(t;z)\right|>\varepsilon
  3122: \right)
  3123: \le 2e^{-2R\varepsilon^2}.
  3124: \]
  3125: Equivalently, for every \(0<\delta<1\), with conditional probability at least
  3126: \(1-\delta\),
  3127: \[
  3128: \sup_t|\widehat F_{N,R}^\ast(t;z)-F_N^\ast(t;z)|
  3129: \le
  3130: \varepsilon_{R,\delta}:=
  3131: \sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2R}} .
  3132: \]
  3133: Let \(q_\alpha=1-\alpha\), let
  3134: \[
  3135: c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)
  3136: =\inf\{t:F_N^\ast(t;z)\ge q_\alpha\},
  3137: \qquad
  3138: \widehat c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z)
  3139: =T_{N,(\lceil q_\alpha R\rceil)}^\ast(z),
  3140: \]
  3141: where the draws are sorted increasingly.  If \(c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)\) is a
  3142: continuity point of \(F_N^\ast(\cdot;z)\), then
  3143: \[
  3144: \widehat c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z)
  3145: \longrightarrow c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)
  3146: \qquad\text{in conditional probability as }R\to\infty .
  3147: \]
  3148: If the conditional law has an atom or a flat stretch at the lower quantile,
  3149: the raw order statistic still estimates a lower conditional quantile in the
  3150: usual empirical-quantile sense, but the raw order statistic at level
  3151: \(q_\alpha\) is not by itself a one-sided coverage certificate.  A DKW
  3152: certificate is obtained by spending probability in the distribution-function
  3153: scale.  If
  3154: \[
  3155: \varepsilon_{R,\delta}<\alpha,\qquad
  3156: q_{\alpha,\delta,R}^{+}:=q_\alpha+\varepsilon_{R,\delta}<1,
  3157: \]
  3158: put \(\widehat c_R=\widehat c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z)\) and define
  3159: \[
  3160: d^{\mathrm{DKW}}_{R,\delta,N}(z)
  3161: :=
  3162: \inf\left\{d\ge0:
  3163: \widehat F_{N,R}^\ast(\widehat c_R+d;z)-\varepsilon_{R,\delta}
  3164: \ge 1-\alpha\right\}.
  3165: \]
  3166: Equivalently, since the empirical distribution is a step function, the
  3167: inflated lower empirical quantile is
  3168: \[
  3169: \widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z)
  3170: :=T_{N,(\lceil q_{\alpha,\delta,R}^{+}R\rceil)}^\ast(z),
  3171: \]
  3172: and \(\widehat c_R+d^{\mathrm{DKW}}_{R,\delta,N}(z)
  3173: =\widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z)\).
  3174: Then, conditionally on the observed record,
  3175: \[
  3176: \PP^\ast\!\left\{
  3177: \widehat c_R+d^{\mathrm{DKW}}_{R,\delta,N}(z)
  3178: \ge c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)\right\}\ge1-\delta .
  3179: \]
  3180: Equivalently, the inflated simulated critical value is the empirical lower
  3181: \((q_\alpha+\varepsilon_{R,\delta})\)-quantile.  This conclusion is
  3182: distribution-free and does not require continuity or a positive density at the
  3183: ideal conditional quantile.
  3184: 
  3185: For the record-probability consequence, fix the true diagonal coordinate
  3186: \(z_0\).  Throughout this paragraph \(\PP\) is the fixed-population law of the
  3187: observed retained record and all record-level gates, whereas \(\PP^\ast\) is
  3188: only the conditional law of the auxiliary Gaussian simulation after that
  3189: record has been observed.  The DKW step below is not a finite-sample
  3190: statement about record coverage; it is a conditional one-sided domination
  3191: statement for the simulated projected-Gaussian critical value.  Let
  3192: \(G_N=G_{\mathrm{cert},N}(z_0)\) be the record-level reporting event on which
  3193: the population branch, ideal-quantile enclosure, and numerical recipe are
  3194: certified.  A finite report may set \(G_N=1\) only when the manuscript or
  3195: attached transcript contains the branch certificate and the ideal conditional
  3196: quantile enclosure used by the reported inequality; otherwise the event is
  3197: declared false for coverage-certification purposes.  Let
  3198: \[
  3199: H_N=G_{\mathrm{MC},N}(z_0)
  3200: =\{\widehat c^-_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z_0)
  3201: +d_{R,\delta_N^{\rm MC},N}(z_0)\ge c^-_{1-\alpha,N}(z_0)\}
  3202: \]
  3203: be the auxiliary one-sided Monte Carlo domination event.  For each realized
  3204: record, \(H_N\) is an event in the auxiliary simulation space; its probability
  3205: is measured by \(\PP^\ast(\,\cdot\,)\), not by \(\PP\).  The event \(H_N\) is
  3206: part of the report only through a displayed one-sided certificate, for example
  3207: the DKW event and inflation in the preceding display.  Suppose
  3208: \[
  3209: \PP(G_N)\to1,\qquad \delta_N^{\rm MC}\to0,
  3210: \]
  3211: and on \(G_N\),
  3212: \[
  3213: \PP^\ast\{\widehat c^-_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z_0)
  3214: +d_{R,\delta_N^{\rm MC},N}(z_0)\ge c^-_{1-\alpha,N}(z_0)\}
  3215: \ge1-\delta_N^{\rm MC} .
  3216: \]
  3217: Define the auxiliary all-space fixed-candidate ideal and simulated diagonal
  3218: acceptance events by
  3219: \[
  3220: \mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0)
  3221: :=\{Q_N(z_0)\le c^-_{1-\alpha,N}(z_0)\},
  3222: \]
  3223: and
  3224: \[
  3225: \mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,aux}}(z_0)
  3226: :=\{Q_N(z_0)\le
  3227: \widehat c^-_{1-\alpha,N,R}(z_0)+d_{R,\delta_N^{\rm MC},N}(z_0)\}.
  3228: \]
  3229: The operational reported events are
  3230: \[
  3231: \mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{rep}}(z_0)
  3232: :=\mathcal E_N\cap\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0),
  3233: \qquad
  3234: \mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,rep}}(z_0)
  3235: :=\mathcal E_N\cap\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,aux}}(z_0).
  3236: \]
  3237: On \(\mathcal E_N^c\) the finite-record procedure withholds the diagonal
  3238: report, so no diagonal finite-record report is formed there.
  3239: Then
  3240: \[
  3241: \PP\{\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,rep}}(z_0)\}
  3242: \ge
  3243: \PP\{\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0)\}
  3244: -\delta_N^{\rm MC}-\PP(G_N^c)-\PP(\mathcal E_N^c)-o(1).
  3245: \]
  3246: Therefore, if the ideal event has limiting coverage \(1-\alpha\), the simulated
  3247: reported event has
  3248: \[
  3249: \liminf_N
  3250: \PP\{\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,rep}}(z_0)\}
  3251: \ge1-\alpha .
  3252: \]
  3253: Without such an explicit event \(G_{\mathrm{MC},N}(z_0)\) and one-sided
  3254: inflation \(d_{R,\delta_N^{\rm MC},N}(z_0)\), finite simulation remains only a
  3255: numerical diagnostic for the ideal conditional projected-Gaussian law.
  3256: \end{lemma}
  3257: 
  3258: \begin{proof}
  3259: Conditional on the record, the variables
  3260: \(T_{N,1}^\ast(z),\ldots,T_{N,R}^\ast(z)\) are independent real-valued draws
  3261: with distribution function \(F_N^\ast(\cdot;z)\).  The displayed exponential
  3262: bound is therefore the Dvoretzky--Kiefer--Wolfowitz inequality, in Massart's
  3263: sharp-constant form, applied under \(\PP^\ast\) \cite{Massart1990DKW}.
  3264: 
  3265: Let
  3266: \[
  3267: \Delta_{N,R}(z)=
  3268: \sup_t|\widehat F_{N,R}^\ast(t;z)-F_N^\ast(t;z)|.
  3269: \]
  3270: The DKW bound gives \(\Delta_{N,R}(z)=O_{\PP^\ast}(R^{-1/2})\).  At a
  3271: continuity point of the conditional quantile, the Glivenko--Cantelli
  3272: consequence of this bound gives the standard empirical-quantile consistency:
  3273: for every \(\eta>0\), after decreasing \(\eta\) if necessary on the left,
  3274: \[
  3275: F_N^\ast(c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)-\eta;z)<q_\alpha
  3276: \le F_N^\ast(c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)+\eta;z).
  3277: \]
  3278: Uniform convergence of \(\widehat F_{N,R}^\ast\) to \(F_N^\ast\) then forces
  3279: the order statistic into this \(\eta\)-neighbourhood with conditional
  3280: probability tending to one.  At a discontinuity or flat quantile the same DKW
  3281: bound controls the simulated distribution function but the uninflated
  3282: \(q_\alpha\)-order statistic need not dominate the ideal lower quantile.
  3283: 
  3284: The DKW-inflated statistic gives a one-sided certificate without any local
  3285: regularity.  On the event
  3286: \[
  3287: B_{R,\delta,N}(z)=
  3288: \left\{\Delta_{N,R}(z)\le\varepsilon_{R,\delta}\right\},
  3289: \]
  3290: we have, with
  3291: \[
  3292: \widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z)
  3293: =T_{N,(\lceil(q_\alpha+\varepsilon_{R,\delta})R\rceil)}^\ast(z),
  3294: \]
  3295: that
  3296: \[
  3297: \widehat F_{N,R}^\ast
  3298: \bigl(\widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z);z\bigr)
  3299: \ge q_\alpha+\varepsilon_{R,\delta}.
  3300: \]
  3301: Therefore
  3302: \[
  3303: F_N^\ast
  3304: \bigl(\widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z);z\bigr)
  3305: \ge q_\alpha .
  3306: \]
  3307: By the definition of the lower quantile,
  3308: \[
  3309: c^{-}_{1-\alpha,N}(z)
  3310: \le
  3311: \widehat c^{\mathrm{DKW},-}_{1-\alpha,N,R,\delta}(z)
  3312: =\widehat c_R
  3313: +d^{\mathrm{DKW}}_{R,\delta,N}(z).
  3314: \]
  3315: Since the DKW inequality gives \(\PP^\ast(B_{R,\delta,N}(z))\ge1-\delta\),
  3316: the stated DKW domination bound follows.  The only deterministic size
  3317: condition needed for this construction is
  3318: \(\varepsilon_{R,\delta}<\alpha\), which ensures that the empirical
  3319: \((q_\alpha+\varepsilon_{R,\delta})\)-quantile is below level one.
  3320: 
  3321: It remains to prove the coverage-transfer inequality.
  3322: On \(G_N\cap H_N\), the auxiliary ideal fixed-candidate event is contained in the
  3323: simulated event, because the simulated critical value is at least as large as
  3324: the ideal one.  Intersecting this inclusion with the reporting gate
  3325: \(\mathcal E_N\) and then taking conditional expectation over the auxiliary
  3326: Monte Carlo draws gives
  3327: \[
  3328: \PP\{\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{MC,rep}}(z_0)\}
  3329: \ge
  3330: \PP\{\mathcal E_N\cap\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0)\cap G_N\}
  3331: -\EE\{1_{G_N}\PP^\ast(H_N^c)\}.
  3332: \]
  3333: The assumed conditional bound on \(G_N\) makes the last term at most
  3334: \(\delta_N^{\rm MC}\).  Since
  3335: \[
  3336: \PP\{\mathcal E_N\cap\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0)\cap G_N\}
  3337: \ge
  3338: \PP\{\mathcal A_N^{\mathrm{aux}}(z_0)\}
  3339: -\PP(G_N^c)-\PP(\mathcal E_N^c),
  3340: \]
  3341: the displayed inequality follows, in fact without the harmless \(o(1)\) term
  3342: except for any asymptotically negligible convention used to make the finite
  3343: Monte Carlo rule measurable on nonreporting records.  The final \(\liminf\)
  3344: claim follows from \(\PP(G_N^c)\to0\), \(\PP(\mathcal E_N^c)\to0\),
  3345: \(\delta_N^{\rm MC}\to0\), and the assumed ideal coverage limit.
  3346: \end{proof}
  3347: 
  3348: \begin{corollary}[DKW simulation domination on the auxiliary product space]\label{cor:dkw-product-space-diagonal-main}
  3349: Fix the certified exchange-diagonal candidate \(z_0\) and condition on the
  3350: retained record.  Let \(P_N\) denote the record law and let \(P_N^\ast\) denote
  3351: the conditional law of the projected-Gaussian simulation used to approximate
  3352: the conditional law of \(T_N^\ast(z_0)\).  Draw \(N\) auxiliary values
  3353: \(T^\ast_1,\ldots,T^\ast_N\) from this conditional law and sort them
  3354: increasingly.  Define
  3355: \[
  3356: \epsilon_N^{MC}=\sqrt{\frac{\log(2N^2)}{2N}}, \qquad
  3357: c_{N,DKW}=T^\ast_{(\lceil(1-\alpha+\epsilon_N^{MC})N\rceil)} .
  3358: \]
  3359: When \(\epsilon_N^{MC}<\alpha\),
  3360: \[
  3361: P_N^\ast\{c_{N,DKW}\ge c^-_{1-\alpha,N}\}\ge1-N^{-2},
  3362: \]
  3363: where \(c^-_{1-\alpha,N}\) is the ideal lower conditional
  3364: \((1-\alpha)\)-quantile for the same retained projected-Gaussian branch.
  3365: Therefore, with
  3366: \[
  3367: A_N^{aux}:=\{Q_N\le c^-_{1-\alpha,N}\},
  3368: \]
  3369: the product-space record-and-simulation statement is
  3370: \[
  3371: (P_N\otimes P_N^\ast)\{E_N\cap[Q_N\le c_{N,DKW}]\}
  3372: \ge
  3373: P_N(A_N^{aux})-P_N(E_N^c)-N^{-2}-o(1).
  3374: \]
  3375: This is only a domination statement for the auxiliary simulated critical
  3376: value.  It is not a bootstrap from the observed record and is not finite-sample
  3377: record coverage; record coverage enters only through the separate fixed-null
  3378: asymptotic theorem for \(A_N^{aux}\) and the retained reporting gate \(E_N\).
  3379: \end{corollary}
  3380: 
  3381: \begin{proof}
  3382: The first display is \Cref{lem:conditional-monte-carlo-quantile-main} with
  3383: \(R=N\), \(\delta=N^{-2}\), and
  3384: \(\epsilon_N^{MC}=\sqrt{\log(2N^2)/(2N)}\).  On the auxiliary domination event
  3385: \(\{c_{N,DKW}\ge c^-_{1-\alpha,N}\}\), the ideal event
  3386: \(A_N^{aux}\) implies \(\{Q_N\le c_{N,DKW}\}\).  Therefore
  3387: \[
  3388: 1_{E_N}1_{A_N^{aux}}1_{\{c_{N,DKW}\ge c^-_{1-\alpha,N}\}}
  3389: \le
  3390: 1_{E_N}1_{\{Q_N\le c_{N,DKW}\}} .
  3391: \]
  3392: Taking first \(P_N^\ast\)-expectation conditional on the record and then
  3393: \(P_N\)-expectation gives
  3394: \[
  3395: (P_N\otimes P_N^\ast)\{E_N\cap[Q_N\le c_{N,DKW}]\}
  3396: \ge
  3397: P_N(E_N\cap A_N^{aux})-N^{-2}-o(1),
  3398: \]
  3399: where the harmless \(o(1)\) allows the usual measurable completion on records
  3400: where the finite reporting protocol withholds the simulation transcript.

  3722: \begin{lemma}[Projected-Gaussian continuity and rank degeneracy]\label{thm:projected-gaussian-continuity-degeneracy-main}
  3723: Fix \(0<z<1\), put
  3724: \[
  3725: n_z=(-z,1)^\top,\qquad H_z=\{h:n_z^\top h\le0\},
  3726: \]
  3727: and let
  3728: \[
  3729: T_z=(\Pi_{H_z}Z)^\top\Sigma^+\Pi_{H_z}Z,
  3730: \qquad Z\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma),
  3731: \]
  3732: where \(\Sigma\) is a nonnegative definite \(2\times2\) matrix.  Then the
  3733: distribution of \(T_z\) is continuous in the following cases:
  3734: \[
  3735: \operatorname{rank}\Sigma=2,
  3736: \qquad\text{or}\qquad
  3737: \operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1
  3738: \text{ and }
  3739: \operatorname{Range}\Sigma\ne \operatorname{span}\{n_z\}.
  3740: \]
  3741: If \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1\) and
  3742: \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma=\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), then
  3743: \[
  3744: \PP(T_z=0)=\frac12
  3745: \]
  3746: and \(T_z\) has no atom on \((0,\infty)\).  More precisely,
  3747: \[
  3748: T_z\sim \frac12\delta_0+\frac12\chi^2_1 .
  3749: \]
  3750: Equivalently,
  3751: \[
  3752: \PP(T_z=0)=\frac12,\qquad
  3753: \PP(T_z\le t)=\frac12+\frac12F_{\chi^2_1}(t),\quad t>0.
  3754: \]
  3755: Consequently, for a lower quantile level \(0<p_c<1\), the lower
  3756: \(p_c\)-quantile is
  3757: \[
  3758: c_{p_c}=0\quad\text{if }p_c\le\frac12,\qquad
  3759: c_{p_c}=F_{\chi^2_1}^{-1}(2p_c-1)\quad\text{if }p_c>\frac12,
  3760: \]
  3761: and the positive-quantile case is a continuity point of the law of \(T_z\).  If
  3762: \(\Sigma=0\), then
  3763: \(T_z=0\) almost surely.  Consequently the continuity condition in
  3764: \Cref{lem:projected-gaussian-bootstrap-halfspace-main} is automatic except
  3765: for the zero covariance case and for the rank-one case in which the retained
  3766: Gaussian direction is normal to the exchange-diagonal tangent half-space; in
  3767: that rank-one normal case the only discontinuity is the atom at zero.
  3768: \end{lemma}
  3769: 
  3770: \begin{proof}
  3771: If \(\Sigma=0\), the assertion is immediate.  Suppose first that
  3772: \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=2\).  The Gaussian law has a Lebesgue density on
  3773: \(\RR^2\).  On \(H_z\), the statistic is the nonzero quadratic form
  3774: \(u^\top\Sigma^+u\).  On \(H_z^c\), the projection is the linear map
  3775: \[
  3776: L_z u=u-\frac{n_z^\top u}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z
  3777: \]
  3778: onto the boundary line \(n_z^\top h=0\), and the statistic is the quadratic
  3779: form \(u^\top L_z^\top\Sigma^+L_z u\), not identically zero on that half-plane.
  3780: For each fixed \(c\), the level set of either nonzero quadratic polynomial has
  3781: Lebesgue measure zero.  Hence \(\PP(T_z=c)=0\) for every \(c\).
  3782: 
  3783: Now let \(\operatorname{rank}\Sigma=1\).  Write
  3784: \(\Sigma=\lambda vv^\top\), where \(\lambda>0\) and \(\|v\|=1\).  Then
  3785: \(Z=\xi v\), with \(\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,\lambda)\), and
  3786: \[
  3787: \Sigma^+=\lambda^{-1}vv^\top .
  3788: \]
  3789: If \(n_z^\top v=0\), the line
  3790: \(\operatorname{span}\{v\}\) lies in the boundary of \(H_z\), so
  3791: \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\), which has a continuous \(\chi^2_1\) law.  If
  3792: \(n_z^\top v\ne0\), one half-line is already in \(H_z\), where again
  3793: \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\).  On the other half-line,
  3794: \[
  3795: \Pi_{H_z}(\xi v)=
  3796: \xi\left(v-\frac{n_z^\top v}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z\right).
  3797: \]
  3798: The corresponding coefficient in the seminorm \(\Sigma^+\) is
  3799: \[
  3800: \lambda^{-1}
  3801: \left\{v^\top\left(v-\frac{n_z^\top v}{\|n_z\|^2}n_z\right)\right\}^2 .
  3802: \]
  3803: This coefficient is positive unless \(v\) is parallel to \(n_z\).  Therefore,
  3804: when \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma\ne\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), \(T_z\) is
  3805: a two-sided mixture of positive multiples of \(\xi^2\) and has no atoms.  When
  3806:   \(\operatorname{Range}\Sigma=\operatorname{span}\{n_z\}\), the outside
  3807:   half-line projects to the origin and contributes mass \(1/2\) at zero, while
  3808:   the inside half-line gives \(T_z=\xi^2/\lambda\).  Conditional on this
  3809:   half-line, \(\xi^2/\lambda\) has the \(\chi^2_1\) law.  Hence the distribution
  3810:   is \(\frac12\delta_0+\frac12\chi^2_1\), and the stated lower-quantile split
  3811:   follows immediately from its distribution function
  3812:   \(F(t)=1/2+\PP\{\chi^2_1\le t\}/2\) for \(t>0\).  This proves the rank-one
  3813:   alternatives.
  3814: \end{proof}
  3815: 
  3816: \begin{proposition}[Computable diagonal covariance]\label{prop:computable-diagonal-covariance-main}
  3817: Work at a fixed constrained D-MAP exchange-diagonal population point
  3818: \(\sigma_z=(2z,z^2)\), \(0<z<1\), equivalently \(p=s=z\).  Put
  3819: \[
  3820: \theta=-\log z.
  3821: \]
  3822: Then the diagonal Palm gap law is
  3823: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-gap-mass-computable-main}
  3824: g_k=\PP(G=k)
  3825: =z^k\{(1-z)(1+\theta k)-\theta z\},\qquad k\ge0 .
  3826: \end{equation}
  3827: In particular
  3828: \[
  3829: g_0=(1-z)-\theta z,\qquad
  3830: g_1=z\{(1-z)(1+\theta)-\theta z\}.
  3831: \]
  3832: Define
  3833: \[
  3834: \mu=\frac1{1-z}+\frac{\theta z}{(1-z)^2},
  3835: \qquad
  3836: \nu=\frac{1+z}{(1-z)^2}
  3837:      +\frac{\theta z(3+z)}{(1-z)^3},
  3838: \qquad
  3839: m_2=g_1+g_0^2 .
  3840: \]
  3841: Then the three inclusion coordinates at this diagonal point are
  3842: \[
  3843: \Rinc(z)=\frac1\mu(1,g_0,m_2)^\top ,
  3844: \]
  3845: and the quotient denominator is
  3846: \[
  3847: \Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}(z)=r_1(z)-r_0(z)^2
  3848: =-\frac{\theta^2z^2(1-z)^2}{(1-z+\theta z)^2}<0 .
  3849: \]
  3850: For the cycle reward variables
  3851: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-cycle-reward-y-main}
  3852: U_j=\left(1,\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0\}},
  3853: \mathbf1_{\{G_j=1\}}+\mathbf1_{\{G_j=0,G_{j+1}=0\}}\right)^\top,
  3854: \qquad
  3855: L_j=G_j+1,\qquad
  3856: Y_j=U_j-\Rinc(z)L_j ,
  3857: \end{equation}
  3858: the \(Y_j\) sequence is one-dependent, centered, and has covariance
  3859: \begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-sigma-r-y-main}
  3860: \Sigma_r(z)=\frac1{\mu(z)}
  3861: \left[\operatorname{Var}(Y_0)+\operatorname{Cov}(Y_0,Y_1)
  3862: +\operatorname{Cov}(Y_1,Y_0)\right].
  3863: \end{equation}
  3864: Every entry in \eqref{eq:diagonal-sigma-r-y-main} is an absolutely convergent
  3865: finite or double sum under the explicit masses \eqref{eq:diagonal-gap-mass-computable-main};
  3866: indeed the indicators in \(U_j\) restrict the nontrivial terms to
  3867: \(G_j\in\{0,1\}\), while the factors containing \(L_j\) are sums of
  3868: polynomials in \(k\) against \(g_k=O(kz^k)\).

  3870: \[
  3871: A_z=
  3872: \begin{pmatrix}
  3873: 1&g_0&m_2\\
  3874: g_0&g_0&g_0^2\\
  3875: m_2&g_0^2&m_2

  3877: \quad

  3883: d_z=
  3884: \begin{pmatrix}
  3885: \mu\\ \mu g_0\\ \mu g_1+g_0^2
  3886: \end{pmatrix},
  3887: \]
  3888: and
  3889: \[
  3890: B_z=

  1234: \begin{lemma}[Exchange symmetry in dimensionless survival coordinates]\label{lem:gap-law-exchange-symmetry-main}
  1235: Put
  1236: \[
  1237: x=-\log p=\Gamma\Delta\tau,\qquad
  1238: y=-\log s=\kappa_{\mathrm r}\Delta\tau,
  1239: \qquad p=e^{-x},\qquad s=e^{-y}.
  1240: \]
  1241: For \(x\ne y\), the zero-run law in \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main} is
  1242: \begin{equation}\label{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}
  1243: g_k=
  1244: \frac{y(1-p)p^k-x(1-s)s^k}{y-x},
  1245: \qquad k\ge0.
  1246: \end{equation}
  1247: The hidden eigenvalue in \eqref{eq:lambda-hid-main} is, in the same
  1248: coordinates,
  1249: \begin{equation}\label{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main}
  1250: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}
  1251: =\frac{y s-x p}{y-x}
  1252: =\frac{y e^{-y}-x e^{-x}}{y-x}.
  1253: \end{equation}
  1254: Both \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main} and
  1255: \eqref{eq:symmetric-lambda-hid-main} are invariant under the simultaneous
  1256: exchange \((x,p)\leftrightarrow(y,s)\).  The equal-rate laws are their
  1257: continuous limits.  Consequently the stationary visible renewal law is
  1258: unchanged by exchanging \(\Gamma\) and \(\kappa_{\mathrm r}\).
  1259: \end{lemma}
  1260: 
  1261: \begin{proof}
  1262: Multiplying numerator and denominator in \eqref{eq:gap-closed-main} by
  1263: \(\Delta\tau\) gives \eqref{eq:symmetric-gap-law-main}.  Similarly,
  1264: \eqref{eq:lambda-hid-main} becomes
  1265: \[
  1266: \lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}
  1267: =\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm r}s-\Gamma p}{\kappa_{\mathrm r}-\Gamma}
  1268: =\frac{y s-x p}{y-x}.
  1269: \]
  1270: Under \((x,p)\leftrightarrow(y,s)\), the numerator
  1271: \[
  1272: y(1-p)p^k-x(1-s)s^k
  1273: \]
  1274: and the denominator \(y-x\) both change sign, so \(g_k\) is unchanged.  The
  1275: same sign cancellation gives
  1276: \[
  1277: y s-x p\longmapsto x p-y s=-(y s-x p),
  1278: \qquad
  1279: y-x\longmapsto x-y=-(y-x),
  1280: \]
  1281: and hence \(\lambda_{\mathrm{hid}}\) is unchanged.  The diagonal formulas
  1282: follow by taking the continuous limits \(x\to y\).  Since
  1283: \Cref{lem:renewal-gap-main} proves that the visible law is the stationary
  1284: renewal law with gap probabilities \(g_k\), the unordered pair
  1285: \(\{(x,p),(y,s)\}\) determines the whole gap distribution.  It also determines
  1286: the stationary renewal intensity \(1/\EE(G+1)\).  Therefore the stationary
  1287: renewal measures generated before and after the exchange have the same
  1288: finite-dimensional visible distributions.
  1289: \end{proof}

  1291: \begin{convention}\label{conv:branch-map-labelled-rates-main}
  1292: \textbf{Identifiable estimands and branch-map convention.}
  1293: From one stationary visible record the identifiable objects are the inclusion
  1294: vector \((r_0,r_1,r_2)\), the quotient
  1295: \(\Phi(r)=(p+s,ps)\) on
  1296: \(\Delta_{\mathrm{inv}}=r_1-r_0^2\ne0\), the unordered survival-factor pair
  1297: \(\{p,s\}\), and visible functionals such as
  1298: \(\gamma_2=\operatorname{Cov}(A_0,A_2)\).  These are quotient-level objects:
  1299: the visible inverse does not order the two roots and does not by itself
  1300: produce labelled attempt/recovery parameters.  When the sampling interval is
  1301: a known design interval, the unordered numerical rate set
  1302: \[
  1303: \left\{-\frac{\log p}{\Delta\tau},
  1304:        -\frac{\log s}{\Delta\tau}\right\}
  1305: \]
  1306: is visible-law identifiable, but the ordered vector
  1307: \((\Gamma,\kappa_{\mathrm r})\) is not.  If \(\Delta\tau\) is not known as a
  1308: design interval, the same quotient identifies only
  1309: \[
  1310: \{-\log p,-\log s\}

     1: \section{Record, inverse, and finite-record results}\label{sec:records}
     2: 
     3: This section gives the record-side results in compressed form.  Each main
     4: result is read under the constrained D-MAP record assumption
     5: \Cref{ass:model-inference-contract-main}, with any extra regularity or
     6: finite-record protocol stated locally.  Between those statements, all
     7: probabilities below are probabilities of the stationary visible binary record
     8: generated by the sampled-counter labelled kernels \(T_0,T_1\).
     9: The killed entries \(T_1(R,R)=T_1(D,R)=0\) are used as assumptions, not as
    10: conclusions; \Cref{prop:model-fragility-boundary-reset-main} states exactly
    11: which renewal and inverse conclusions are lost for same-bin recovery leakage.
    12: Throughout this section \((A_n)_{n\in\mathbb Z}\) denotes that two-sided
    13: stationary extension.
    14: One-sided strings such as \(A_0,\ldots,A_{N-1}\) are finite restrictions used
    15: only for observed-record statistics; when a proof conditions on a past event
    16: or shifts a renewal epoch, it is using the same stationary two-sided process.

    18: \subsection{Model and renewal law}
